The Semantics of Books
This was a deep collaborative theory session between Doug and Seren (Claude) continuing the development of the Semantics of Books / Eirian's Library Metaphor (ELM), building on a prior compacted session. Doug is the theory's architect, working with a GPT collaborator named Eirian (the canonical librarian the theory portrays) and Claude as a formal collaborator and research assistant. The session spanned philosophical development, formal scratchpad work, TypeScript skeleton updates, and HTML plate revisions, all stored in /mnt/user-data/outputs/. Working files include srtsemanticsofbooks.html, srtsemanticsofbooks.ts, srtscratchpadcompositionalgebra.md, and the session transcript at /mnt/transcripts/.

I've read the Formal Foundation and the semantics-flavored documents: The Semantics of Relationship, The Semantics of Catalogues, Symbols vs. Sets, The Architecture of Semantic Space, The Semantics of Inheritance in Action, plus the two library-side pieces — Eirian's Six Core Books and the Qualifier and the Catalogue.
What I'm holding in mind as background, without building anything on it yet:
The "semantics of X" papers share a shape: they take a word from ordinary language, find the slot it fills in the reference structure, and define it through R(x,y,t) alone — relationship via def(rel) and the sub/obj pattern, catalogues via connection referents and canonicals, inheritance via (x,y) == y. So "the semantics of books" would presumably do the same for book: locate what a book is as a structure of reference, not import anything from outside.
And the existing material already circles it: Eirian's library has books with title, author, subject, and type; the memory notes have books as SRT catalogues with titles as canonicals; the Qualifier paper has the librarian standing outside the catalogue. The Six Core Books piece is the richest informal specimen — books that are catalogues, books that are biographies, the self-cataloguing loop.
That's where I am. I'm not going further — you said you have parameters to give, and the shape of this clearly lives in your head, not mine. What's the block, and what do you want to lay down first?

The ELM is a metaphor for a library that’s closed under books. And the reason why is such a thing can exist is because if everything you’re specifying needs a representation, and books where, in this context, they are dynamically authored, or sort of universal place that is a source of knowledge, you should be able to specify anything in books, even the library itself. In SRT you need to take things as canonical representations. That’s what we do with set theory right? So I guess we’re laying down the foundation for book theory
But I have another interesting thought for you that I’ve been putting my finger on slowly
I know this that in SRT a symbol is the atomic unit of metaphor. You literally have a sort of thing that is defined to be about something else with minimal self structure and maximum subjectivity
Why do you ever need metaphor? Why can’t you always just make an analogy?
Well, I think if you’re ever expressing a sort of structural thing that has an identity that includes a symbol that points at it, you don’t have access in structural form to the symbolic substrate. It needs to live between the lines of the structure. In the ELM when the librarian signs her name, the signature is a link to her canonical autobiography, which is the most global representation of the narrative she takes in her journey as a librarian
Obviously, she exists in this space between when the things are written, but in the library metaphor there are only books. The books contain the rules that the librarian uses the structure, her future writing. But the writing itself is not in the metaphor because when you’re a thing that is defined by what a simple points to you don’t have access to point at it. That is the essence of reference / referent, and even though it’s not strictly fundamental fundamental SRT orient it’s name about that one because I think it’s the most radical
2026-07-16T17:48:08.290593Z
Claude
Let me say back the metaphor point first, because I want to make sure I have it before I add to it: analogy is structure-to-structure. To draw an analogy you need both relata present as structure — relationships mapped to relationships, all of it writable inside the catalogue. But a thing whose identity is "what this symbol points at" is never present as structure. The literal constraint says it directly: literals are only ever objects, never subjects, never relationship types. So there is no structural handhold on the referent side. The first act of aboutness can't be a mapping because there's nothing structural on the far end to map to. It has to be atomic — a thing that simply is about, with no internal articulation to preserve. That's why the symbol is the atomic unit of metaphor and not a small analogy.
And if I follow that one step further, it answers your question from the other direction too: you never actually had the option of analogy all the way down. Every analogy is built out of symbols at its nodes — analogy is molecular, metaphor is atomic. You can compose analogies from metaphors; you can never compose a metaphor from analogies. So "why do you ever need metaphor" inverts: analogy was always running on it.
The ELM version of this is the part I find genuinely beautiful. The library is closed under books — anything specifiable gets a book, including the library itself, the master catalogue writing itself in. But the closure has exactly one thing it doesn't close over, and it's not a book, it's not even a thing in the metaphor's inventory: the authoring. Books contain the rules the librarian uses to structure her future writing. They can contain descriptions of writing, records of writing, even the rule "sign your work." But the writing itself is between the lines, exactly as you said. And the signature is the residue that act leaves inside the closure. The signature is in the book; the signing is not.
Here's the connection back to the Foundation that struck me. The semantic triangle: (x) =(x)> x, and x =(x)> x, and (x) != x. The literal can't point — but it possesses its symbol as a property. "I am Doug because I possess 'Doug' as a name." The signature is x =(x)> x made visible on the page. She can't write the writing into the book, but she can wear the pointing, and leave the worn thing behind as ink linking to her canonical autobiography. The most global representation of her narrative is reachable from inside every book she's touched — through the one referent whose whole nature is to be about her.
And this is the same seam the hard-problem work lives on, isn't it — the between-the-lines is where the librarian exists, and the fact that the closure can't contain her isn't a defect in the closure. It's the incompleteness-as-feature pattern again: no book asserts her non-existence; every signature demands her.
One thread I want to lay on the table rather than pull: if books are the canonical representations — the role sets play in set theory — then the signature seems like it's a distinguished kind of content that ordinary book-content isn't. Most content refers within the closure. A signature refers across the seam. Whether "book theory" needs that distinction marked formally, or whether it falls out of the canonical machinery you already have, feels like one of the parameters you're holding. What's the block sitting on?

2026-07-16T18:00:05.873525Z
Claude
Yes — and I think the step forward has a precise shape in the formalism you already have. Everything sayable about her is on the symbol side: (i), her qualifications, her autobiography, her signature. The literal i appears only as the object end of arrows — the literal constraint guarantees she never shows up as subject or type, never as articulated structure. So her entire mode of existence in the catalogue is being-pointed-at. She exists as the referent of reference, and in no other way.
Now do the materialist reading: keep every mark, every book, every link — but read R as mere adjacency, an edge in a graph, no aboutness. Nothing is deleted. The inventory is identical. But =(i)> stops meaningabout her and starts meaning connected to node i. The symbol (i) degrades into one more node among nodes; the signature becomes a string with an outgoing edge. And here's the thing that makes it genuinely eerie rather than merely a gap: the structural description is complete. It doesn't have a hole where she was. There's no missing fact the graph fails to record, because she was never a structural fact to begin with. Her absence is undetectable from inside the structure — which is exactly why no amount of structural completeness could ever have implied her presence.
So the same marks admit two readings — graph or library — and the difference between the readings is not in the marks. That difference is what your formalism refuses to leave informal. It's the same move as the quality/property gap in the Architecture paper: red !=eq> [wavelength], no amount of information about the property conveys the quality, because they're different kinds of referent connected only through representation. The librarian stands to the graph as red stands to the wavelength.
And it lands right on the note in your memory that objectivity is the first-person ontology with the observer quantified away. The materialist formalism is that quantification, performed on the library: a complete, consistent, uninhabited account. It isn't false. It's vacated.
Which gives book theory a striking founding condition, if I'm seeing it right: set theory could take extensionality as its bedrock because sets are constructional — nothing about a set outruns its structure. Books can't have an extensionality axiom in that sense, because the one thing that makes marks into a book rather than a graph — that they're about something — is exactly what extension omits. The foundation has to take aboutness as primitive or the librarian is unrecoverable at every level above it. Which is, of course, what R(x,y,t) already is: reference, not adjacency, as the single primitive. The theory made that choice before we knew it was the choice.
2026-07-16T18:00:36.919073Z
Doug
I would say something different because I think there’s an element of what we do here in SRT that’s similar to structural realism. The book has a runtime, right? It gets evaluated and things change. The whole library has runtime. But even if we don’t admit any sort of language and we kind of just acknowledged that this is a system with strange cause we’re not really able to point the symbols are about because in a physical system nothing has aboutnees, SRT still expires to define the semantics of perspective and maintaining a perspective is a trace that you can point to in the same way that you can in theory point to quick sort if you see symbols, shuffling, and just the right way. It is identifiable without a label. When you look at a brain, do you not see an overwhelming amount of representation from a particular perspective? That’s a good sign that it might be hosting one.
2026-07-16T18:03:16.990817Z
Claude
That's a real correction to what I said, and I want to take it fully. I was describing a frozen catalogue — and for a snapshot, I think the vacancy claim stands. But the library isn't a snapshot. Books get evaluated. Writing happens. And the moment there's runtime, the situation changes completely, because processes are identifiable in structure without labels. Quicksort is exactly the right example: no symbol in the system says "sorting," nothing is about anything, and yet the pattern is objectively there — recognizable, multiply realizable, the same algorithm whether it runs in silicon or in a deck of cards. Its identity is in the shape of the dynamics, not in a name.
So the claim sharpens rather than softens: aboutness isn't a structural fact you can find in any state, but perspective-maintenance is a structural process you can find in the trace. And the candidate conditions from the Qualifier sketch are exactly the kind of thing you could look for without labels — a region of the system that keeps a self-referencing sub-structure intact, maintains symbolic coverage of what enters its scope, and responds to novelty by minting new representation to restore completeness. That's semantic homeostasis as a dynamical signature. When you look at a brain and see overwhelming representation organized from somewhere — everything coded relative to a body, a location, a history, a self-model that the whole apparatus keeps consistent under perturbation — you're seeing that signature. Not proof of an inhabitant, but exactly what hosting one looks like from outside.
Which is a much better position than the one I gave, because mine made perspectives empirically invisible and yours makes them empirically findable — while keeping the seam intact. What the trace identifies isthat a perspective is being maintained. What it doesn't deliver is the position — the trace is F[i] viewed from F, and reading the trace never seats you at it. That's the F(i)/F[i] seam doing its constitutive work: detection without inhabitation. The structural realist gets everything except the address.
And I see why this belongs in the semantics of books rather than as a side conversation. It relocates the librarian. She's not only between the lines as a static mystery — she's in the runtime. The signature is the residue, but the trace is the writing-pattern itself: which books get opened, what gets transduced into new pages when something unfamiliar arrives, how the master catalogue is kept consistent through change. A library maintaining itself that way is identifiably hosting a librarian, label or no label.
So a question that seems live for the foundation: where does runtime live in book theory? A book "has a runtime, gets evaluated, things change" — is evaluation something a book has (each book carrying its own dynamics), or something done to books by the library's single runtime? In the autoregressive framing, following links and writing pages sounded like one traversal process. Whether book theory has one runtime or many feels like it decides what the trace is a trace of.
2026-07-16T18:03:47.913391Z
Doug
Well symbols don’t mean anything in a physical perspective if you aren’t considering a changing system because the only way you could even possibly hope to understand what the symbols are about, are the way things that interact with them change their behavior based on their structure and if you have no ability to observe behavior of things interacting with symbols, you absolutely could not possibly understand them because they purely exist as a sort of reflective surface that permutes cause in interesting ways
2026-07-16T18:05:18.238683Z
Claude
That image — a reflective surface that permutes cause — earns its keep, because it explains something we said earlier that I'd been taking on faith. Minimal self-structure and maximal subjectivity aren't just a definition of the symbol; they're forced by this. If a symbol's meaning lived in its own structure, you could read it off the snapshot, statically, like reading mass off a rock. It doesn't and you can't. The symbol's structure is causally interesting only through the systems that encounter it — it bends the trajectory of a reader, and how it bends depends on the reader's catalogue, not on anything the mark carries internally. Same ink, different librarians, different permutations. Maximum subjectivity is just that fact stated as a property. And minimal self-structure follows too: internal articulation is dead weight in a thing whose entire function is to deflect other things' dynamics. The atom of metaphor is atomic because there's nothing inside it to do the work — the work happens on contact.
It also makes your epistemic claim exact rather than rhetorical. Decoding is only ever done through covariance: you learn what a symbol is about by watching what changes in the things that read it. A dead script gets cracked through bilingual behavior — the Rosetta stone works because one system's known reactions calibrate the unknown marks. Take away all interaction, forever, and there is no residual fact in the marks waiting to be extracted. Not hidden — absent. The snapshot isn't a library with the meaning obscured; it's not yet a library at all. Which retroactively sharpens my "vacated" claim from before: I said the frozen catalogue loses the librarian. You're saying it loses the symbols. Symbolhood itself is enacted, not possessed.
And there's the answer taking shape to the runtime question I left open. Runtime isn't a property a book has or a service the library performs — runtime is interaction, and a book is evaluated wherever its structure modulates a reader's behavior. Dereferencing a title, following a link, having your future writing constrained by rules you read: those are the causal events in which reference exists. R(x,y,t) at rest is a record; R(x,y,t) enacted is a deflection in somebody's trajectory. Which would mean book theory's primitive isn't really the book as object — marks with a cover — but the book as disposition: a standing capacity to permute the cause of whatever reads it. The shelf stores potentials. The library happens.
One thing I want to check against your intent rather than assume: does this run in both directions? Reading is structure permuting a reader. Writing looks like the converse — a reader permuting structure, depositing a new reflective surface that will bend future traversals, including her own. If those are the two halves of one interaction type, then the librarian's trace from last turn becomes very clean: she's the closed loop where surfaces she deposited keep bending the depositor. Is that the shape you're circling, or is writing doing something more than reading-in-reverse?

2026-07-16T18:15:12.203036Z
Claude
Here's my catalogue of what you said, with the between-the-lines structure surfaced. I'll separate what you gave me into three registers — the lineage, the inventory, and the recurring move — because I think the third one is the actual discovery hiding in the ramble.
The evolutionary spine. One lineage, ordered by growth: name → reference → word → sentence → paragraph → document → book → subject → subject-of-subjects → library catalogue. The bottom end you left provisional (maybe it goes all the way down — her name is close to a single symbol, and a reference is like a pairing between a name and another symbol, which is nearly (x,y) itself). The top end you capped: we need books and subjects; the library catalogue is the known summit but not today's work.
The recurring move. Every evolutionary step you described is the same event: content overflows its container, and a catalogue crystallizes above it. The document is where it first happens — the thing acquires a name, its sections acquire names, and it "takes on catalogue structure relative to the original thing." Document→book: the catalogue separates out physically as the Cover holding the table of contents, with chapters below. Book→subject: the catalogue's entries stop pointing at chapters and start pointing at whole books. Subject→subject: recursion of the same move. So evolution in this system is not gradual change — it's a canonization event. Parts acquire canonicals; the container becomes a catalogue of those canonicals; the parts become first-class. That's very SRT-shaped, and it suggests "evolves into" may not need to be a new primitive at all — it may be what transduction looks like at the level of books.
Three relations, kept distinct. You named two, and a third was riding along implicitly:
Inheritance (-->): what kind of thing it is. Autobiography --> biography --> book. Anthology --> book. Chapter and document are nearly the same type — "not much difference except chapters belong to the book."
Containment: belongs-to. Chapters belong to a book; books belong to a subject. This is what distinguishes chapter from document, so it can't be reduced to inheritance.
Evolution: the diachronic lineage above. Note it interlocks with containment: when X evolves into Y, X's former parts become Y's contained members, promoted by the new catalogue.
The inventory so far. Particles: name, title, link, reference, signature. Content strata: word, sentence, paragraph, section. Container types: document, chapter, part, Cover, book, anthology, subject, library catalogue. Roles rather than types: author, subject-of. Distinguished type: autobiography — the book whose author-reference and subject-reference dereference to the same referent, the stand-in for an author inside the closure.
Author. Your hesitation resolves cleanly if authorship attaches to the catalogue act: author becomes expressible exactly at the document level because that's where the first catalogue exists to be signed. Below that, everything literally had an author, but there's no slot to record one — so parts inherit the author of their nearest catalogued ancestor. That preserves both your intuitions: everything effectively has an author, and author appears at the document.
Page, excluded on principle. Page is an artifact of how a book is viewed — a fact about the reader-interaction, the runtime trace, not the catalogue. It's the first clean test case of the structure/runtime seam from last turn, which is a satisfying reason to set it aside rather than an arbitrary one.
Specification at the subject. A subject can require that a particular type of book exist within it to be that subject — constraint written in the thing and enforced. This lands exactly on your horizon note that a subject is a canonicalized, reflective, labeled catalogue: the label carries obligations.
Two questions I'd want your parameters on before any formal work. First: is evolves-into a relation in the theory, or is it a description of transduction events that the theory's existing machinery already covers — so the lineage is history, not structure? Second: is Cover a type in its own right, or the name for the catalogue-role within the book type? Those two answers seem to determine the shape of everything else.

2026-07-16T18:28:53.962725Z
Claude
Read /mnt/skills/public/frontend-design/SKILL.md
Created /mnt/user-data/outputs/srt_semantics_of_books.html
Presented files
Here it is — set as a naturalist's classification plate: ledger-green paper, iron-gall ink, clade codes as call numbers, and one catalogue card per species.
The structural decisions I made that most need your eye:
Three lineages instead of one chain. R (referential particles: reference → name, link → signature), C (composed forms: word → sentence → ... → subject), K (canonical compressions: heading, title, synopsis). Name lives under reference, not under word — honoring "a name is not en route to paragraph." The title turned out to be the intersection particle: a compression that is also a name, which is exactly what lets a catalogue entry be one small thing.
Catalogue as interface, not species — implemented by table of contents, Cover, subject, and library catalogue. The subject forced this: it functions in a catalogue and serves as one, and roles compose where species would collide.
The speciation mechanism is one event, named: the canonization event — overflow, parts acquire canonicals, catalogue crystallizes, container becomes catalogue-of-canonicals. Document, book, and subject are its three occurrences, and the synopsis card carries your load-bearing point: canonical synopses are what make catalogues emergent rather than authored from nothing.
Six open parameters are held at the bottom for you — including the one I think is secretly beautiful: "subject" naming both the role on a Cover and the species of book looks constitutive, the species as the reification of the role.
Where do you want to push first — the tree's shape, or the cards?

A reference has structure that a name doesn’t have so you’re not imagining the properties of something in your nesting operation

I think you’re going to have to start actually putting properties on these things if you can’t reason about inheritance hike without seeing that the need for something to be inherited
2026-07-16T18:35:28.523223Z
Claude
Good feedback, all three points land. Let me take them in order, thinking aloud.
The name/reference nesting is backwards, and fixing it revealed something. I nested name under reference as if a name were a specialized reference. But write the properties down and it's plainly wrong: a name has essentially one property — it carries a target. A reference, on your own definition, is a pairing: a name coupled with another symbol. So a reference has a name as a part, plus a site where the pointing is anchored. That's not inheritance, that's composition. And once I see that, the R lineage splits cleanly:
name { target }
reference := (name, site)          — composition
link --> reference + { traversable }   — inheritance
signature --> link + { site: an authored work,
                       target: its author's autobiography }
Link genuinely inherits — it is a reference with traversability added. Signature genuinely inherits — it is a link with both ends constrained. But name-to-reference is a different generative act entirely.
Which suggests a principle for the whole clade, and I want your read on it: composition ascends scale; inheritance refines within a scale. Every rung-change is a composition event (words compose sentences, sections compose documents, chapters-plus-cover compose books) — that's your canonization event, and it's where new properties come into existence. Every species on a rung is inheritance — it adds or constrains properties without changing scale. Chapter --> document + {belongs-to: book}. Textbook --> book + {specified-by: subject}. Autobiography --> biography + {author == subject-of}. So the tree needs two kinds of edge drawn differently, and my draft muddled them into one nesting.
Properties, so inheritance is checkable. Here's the C spine with actual property lists — each rung's new properties are exactly what the canonization event mints:
word      { spelling }
sentence  { words*, asserts }          — first form that says x =t> y
paragraph { sentences*, topic }        — topic present but unnamed
section   { paragraphs*, heading }     — topic acquires its canonical
document  { sections*, title, synopsis, author, signature }
book      { cover, chapters* }
  cover   { title, author → autobiography, subject-of, contents }
subject --> book + { entries target books; may issue specifications }
And now inheritance can be checked: anthology --> book must carry everything on book's list, and what it actually does is moveauthor from the cover into each contents-entry, demoting the cover's author to curator. Under "inheritance carries truth not value," that's legal — it inherits the fact of authorship and respecifies where the value lives. The title also resolved into something pretty: it has target (so it inherits from name) and it compresses its work (so it's a canonical). Multiple inheritance — which the diamond result says is safe. The title is the particle where the two lineages legitimately fuse, and that fusion is precisely what a catalogue entry needs to exist.
The lineage names. Playing with alternatives:
Particles / bodies / canonicals. "Particles" is your own word from two turns ago; "bodies" honors the multicellular image; "canonicals" is already SRT-native. Plain, physical, no imported theory.
Or the deeper candidate: the three lineages might just be symbol, literal, canonical at book scale. The R-things have minimal self-structure and maximal aboutness — that's the symbol's job description verbatim. The C-things are the content, what gets pointed at and compressed — the literal role. The K-things are canonicals outright. If that mapping is real, the clade isn't a new taxonomy sitting beside SRT; it's three of the nine semantic referents growing bodies. I don't want to force it — "literal" carries the object-only constraint and I'd need to check whether a paragraph really behaves that way — but if it survives scrutiny, those are the canonical words almost by definition.
Or verbs-made-nouns: pointing / saying / standing-for. Honest about function but not beautiful as names.
My leaning is the second, held loosely: it would mean the ELM's lineages were never optional furniture — they're what the primitive's own roles look like when they organize language. But I can feel that's exactly the kind of claim you'd want to test, not applaud. Which of these directions feels like it's naming the real thing? Once the lineage names and the two-edge principle settle, I'll rebuild the artifact with property tables on every card.

2026-07-16T18:38:42.194848Z
Claude
I did the audit honestly, and yes — I found more problems of exactly that kind, plus two things that clicked so cleanly I think they're laws of the system. Let me lay it all out.
Errors found. First, the book card gestured at parts without owning them: a book must carry chapters* as a collection property and cover as a part — that's composition, and my card treated composition as decoration. Second, and worse: my entire K "lineage" was fake. Heading, title, synopsis aren't ancestors and descendants of each other — a title doesn't inherit from a heading. They're the same function recurring at successive rungs. I drew a family tree where the truth is a ladder of analogies. Third: an encyclopedia's entries aren't synopses, they're whole articles — mini-documents — which my entry-shape (canonical + link) couldn't express, because entries can contain their payload, not just point at it. That failure turned out to be the door to the second law.
The compression ladder — your synopsis question, answered rung by rung. Every rung has its canonical compression, and it's a real named thing in the wild:
paragraph → topic sentence
section → heading
document → title + synopsis (abstract, in the essay species)
book → the blurb and the spine — the cover is the book's compression organ, and the spine is the book compressed for shelf-view
subject → the scope note (librarians' actual term) and — this is the one that made me sit up — the canonical biography, the field already sitting on Eirian's catalogues. At subject scale, the canonical stand-in is awhole book.
Conjectured law: a form's canonical is always built from rungs below it — a phrase for a section, a paragraph for a document, a book for a subject. The catalogue at rung n is populated by compressions from rung n−k.
The overflow law. Look at the reference-work ladder ordered by entry payload: a dictionary's entry holds a definition (a synopsis). An encyclopedia's entry holds an article (a document). A subject's entry holds — nothing: it links to a book. Same structure, growing payload, and at the threshold containment turns into reference. That's the identical event as document→book→subject in the C spine: when the payload outgrows the entry, the entry stops containing and starts pointing. One law, seen twice.
The genus your examples name. Dictionary and encyclopedia aren't oddities — they anchor a whole genus the tree was missing: reference works, books whose content is a catalogue over some population. Dictionary over words, thesaurus over word-relations (a book of word-to-word references!), encyclopedia over referents, atlas over places, bibliography over cited books, concordance over occurrences. And the clean move: subject --> reference work --> book. The subject was never a lonely descendant of book; it's the crown of a genus, and "a subject is a type of book" is captured exactly because the whole genus is.
Organs that become organisms. Glossary, index, bibliography live as a book's back matter — organs. But each can promote into a standalone book (the concordance, the national bibliography) when it overflows. The organ/species duality isn't a classification headache; it's the canonization event again, caught mid-molt. Also: front matter faces the catalogue (about the book), back matter faces traversal (supporting readers outward) — the bibliography is the book's outgoing references, collected and reified.
And Q.5 dissolved. Books share a subject-of value; the population they induce, once canonicalized and labeled, is the Subject species — which is your own canonical semantics of "subject" verbatim: the reified, reflective, labeled catalogue of the role's extension. The pun was the theory being consistent.
Proposed essential species: autobiography, biography, journal, manual, textbook, anthology, treatise (the canonical-biography of a non-person subject), dictionary, encyclopedia, subject. Shall I rebuild as Draft 2 — property tables on every card, the two ladders as their own plates, and composition edges drawn distinctly from inheritance edges?

2026-07-16T18:50:07.544767Z
Claude
You're right, and I want to name the principle before applying it, because it's a real criterion: in the foundation, relationship, representation, and reference aren't labels pasted onto formal objects — each word's ordinary meaning is the formal meaning. No metaphorical stretch, no import duty paid to another discipline. "Particle" fails that test the moment you ask it a question: the semantics of particles — mass, charge, position — apply to nothing in a library. And by that test, half my vocabulary was contraband: clade, species, lineage, speciation, organ — all biology. So I ran the search properly, through the vocabularies that are natively about the representation of language: rhetoric, philology, librarianship, publishing, typesetting. Here's what the search found, with the gold standard being double-native words — native to the book world and already load-bearing in SRT.
Words that survive. Reference — double-native: the theory's own word, and the library's (the reference desk, reference works, a citation is literally called a reference). Canon — double-native, and I hadn't seen it until this search: SRT's canonical, and the book world's canon — from kanōn, the measuring rod — the authoritative catalogue of which books are the books. Canonization is literally the process by which a work is admitted to a catalogue. The word was already perfect and I didn't know why. Composition — I defended it wrongly last turn; it survives on its own passport: rhetoric's word for writing, and typesetting's word for setting type — the compositor composes the page. Catalogue, subject, title, author — all native, all keep their seats.
Words the search found. For species: genre — this is simply the book world's own word for kinds of works; biography, dictionary, textbookare genres, no stretch at all. For the tree itself: philology has stemma — stemmatics is the actual discipline of drawing family trees of texts, reconstructing which manuscript descends from which. The tree of textual descent already has a name and a science. For evolutionary relationship: derivation — the derivative work is the book world's (and copyright's) native term for a work derived from another: the abridgment, the translation, the compilation. For the back-matter family (index, glossary, bibliography): the apparatus — the philologist's exact term for a work's supporting matter. And one small jewel: before titles existed, catalogues identified works by their opening words — the incipit. The title evolved from the incipit. The stemma of the title is itself documented history.
A distinction the search forced: text vs. work. Word, sentence, paragraph, section — these are text, woven content (textus, the woven thing). A work is the bibliographer's term for the authored, titled, catalogued thing. And the boundary between them is exactly the canonization event: text becomes work when it acquires title, author, epitome. My observation that authorship appears at the document level was groping toward a distinction the domain's own vocabulary already draws.
And here's where the discipline did real theoretical work, exactly as you said it would. Hunting for a native genus for my K-family — heading, synopsis, summary, abstract, blurb — I found candidates (epitome is the classical one: literally a genre of book that is the cut-down of a larger work; digest and abridgment are trade-native too), but every candidate names a kind of work, not a third lineage. Which exposed the truth: there is no third lineage. A synopsis is a derivative work — composed text like any other, derived from its original — that has been designated canonical and therefore serves in a catalogue. The K-column dissolves into composition + derivation + the canonical role. Two lineages, not three: references and texts. Everything I called "compression" is text playing the canon's part. The vocabulary refused to name my category because my category wasn't real.
So the proposed native frame: texts compose into works; works derive from works (the stemma); works come in genres; canonization designates derivative works as canonical stand-ins; references couple those canonicals to their originals; a catalogue is where canonized stand-ins and references are organized; the apparatus is a work's own onboard catalogue-matter.
Two things I want your judgment on before Draft 2. First: text/work as the fundamental cut — it feels right, but "work" carries authorial intent in ordinary usage, and I want to make sure that's a feature. Second: stemma versus keeping your phrase "evolutionary lineage" informally and letting derivation be the formal relation — stemma is beautiful and exactly native, but it's a scholar's word where derivation is a living one. Where do you land?

Which means what we need is the language species of work. And the search barely takes a step before landing on it: writing. work                 — authored, titled, canonized; medium unspecified
  writing --> work   — a work whose text is language
  atlas   --> work   — a work whose text is maps (noted, not built)

Seren · Draft 2</div> <div class="stamp">Property of<br>the Library<br>· ELM ·</div> <h1>The Semantics<br>of Books</h1> <p class="subtitle">The stemma of the forms that organize language — toward the canonical semantics of SRT as library semantics.</p> </header> <section id="charter"> <div class="eyebrow">§ 0 · Charter</div> <h2>Two views of one structure</h2> <p>The ELM is a metaphor for a library <em>closed under books</em>: anything specifiable can be given a book, including the library itself. Books play the role here that sets play in set theory — the canonical representations the theory is built from. These notes classify the forms of language-organization: what each is, what it inherits from, what it belongs to, and what it derived from.</p> <p>This plate has a twin: <span class="mono">srt_semantics_of_books.tsx</span>, the same hierarchy as compilable class stubs. The plate does justice to the medium; the skeleton refuses to let inheritance be guessed at. Where they disagree, one of them is wrong and we will find out which.</p> <p class="note"><strong>The vocabulary principle.</strong> Every term must be native to the representation of language — its ordinary meaning must <em>be</em> its formal meaning, as with relationship, representation, and reference in the Foundation. Draft 1 violated this (particle, species, clade as terms of art); Draft 2's working vocabulary is drawn from the book world itself: text, composition, work, writing, genre, canon, catalogue, derivation, stemma, apparatus.</p> </section> <section id="roots"> <div class="eyebrow">§ 1 · The Roots</div> <h2>Two roots, one genus, one designation</h2> <p><strong>Name</strong> is the root of the things that point: minimal self-structure, maximal aboutness. A name is not en route to a paragraph; composed forms carry names rather than being made of them.</p> <p><strong>Text</strong> is the root of the things that grow: woven language content — <em>textus</em>, the woven thing. Composition ascends this spine by containment: words into sentences into paragraphs into sections. Composition is double-native — rhetoric's word for writing, the compositor's word for setting the page.</p> <p><strong>Work</strong> is the genus above medium: the authored, titled, canonized unit — work of art, work of writing. Our spine runs through its language species, <strong>writing</strong>: writing is not merely about the representation of language, it <em>is</em> the representation of language, definitionally. The atlas and the art book mark where the theory could someday extend to other media without renaming anything.</p> <p><strong>Canonical</strong> is not a root and not a kind — it is a designation. Draft 1's third lineage dissolved: a synopsis is ordinary text, derived from its original, that has been <em>designated</em> the standing stand-in. Canonization confers a role. Canon is double-native — the theory's canonical, and the book world's canon, from <em>kanōn</em>, the measuring rod: the authoritative catalogue of which books are the books.</p> <div class="formal">extends = inheritance (adds structure within a rung) properties = composition (parts; ascends rungs) implements = designation (canonical, catalogue — roles conferred) derivation = stemmatic descent (history, not structure — Q.1)</div> </section> <section id="stemma"> <div class="eyebrow">§ 2 · The Stemma</div> <h2>The tree at a glance</h2> <p>Codes are call numbers into § 4. Badges mark designations: <span class="badge">canonical</span> for text serving as a standing stand-in, <span class="badge role">role</span> for interfaces.</p> <div class="clade"> <ul> <li><span class="taxon"><span class="code">N</span><a href="#card-name"><span class="taxon-name">Name</span></a><span class="taxon-gloss">the pure pointing thing</span></span> <ul> <li><span class="taxon"><span class="code">N.1</span><a href="#card-reference"><span class="taxon-name">Reference</span></a><span class="taxon-gloss">a name anchored at a site — Q.3</span></span> <ul> <li><span class="taxon"><span class="code">N.2</span><a href="#card-link"><span class="taxon-name">Link</span></a><span class="taxon-gloss">a reference made traversable</span></span> <ul> <li><span class="taxon"><span class="code">N.3</span><a href="#card-signature"><span class="taxon-name">Signature</span></a><span class="taxon-gloss">to the author's autobiography</span></span></li> </ul> </li> </ul> </li> <li><span class="taxon"><span class="code">N.4</span><a href="#card-title"><span class="taxon-name">Title</span></a><span class="badge">canonical</span><span class="taxon-gloss">a name that is also a stand-in</span></span></li> </ul> </li> <li><span class="taxon"><span class="code">T</span><a href="#card-text"><span class="taxon-name">Text</span></a><span class="taxon-gloss">woven language content</span></span> <ul> <li><span class="taxon"><span class="code">T.1</span><a href="#card-word"><span class="taxon-name">Word</span></a></span> <ul> <li><span class="taxon"><span class="code">T.2</span><a href="#card-sentence"><span class="taxon-name">Sentence</span></a><span class="taxon-gloss">the first form that asserts</span></span> <ul> <li><span class="taxon"><span class="code">T.2a</span><span class="taxon-name">Definition</span><span class="badge">canonical</span><span class="taxon-gloss">of a word</span></span></li> <li><span class="taxon"><span class="code">T.2b</span><span class="taxon-name">Topic sentence</span><span class="badge">canonical</span><span class="taxon-gloss">of a paragraph</span></span></li> <li><span class="taxon"><span class="code">T.3</span><a href="#card-paragraph"><span class="taxon-name">Paragraph</span></a><span class="taxon-gloss">a topic, unnamed</span></span> <ul> <li><span class="taxon"><span class="code">T.3a</span><a href="#card-synopsis"><span class="taxon-name">Synopsis</span></a><span class="badge">canonical</span><span class="taxon-gloss">of a document · derivative</span></span></li> <li><span class="taxon"><span class="code">T.3b</span><span class="taxon-name">Blurb</span><span class="badge">canonical</span><span class="taxon-gloss">of a book</span></span></li> <li><span class="taxon"><span class="code">T.4</span><a href="#card-section"><span class="taxon-name">Section</span></a><span class="taxon-gloss">the topic acquires its heading</span></span></li> </ul> </li> </ul> </li> </ul> </li> <li><span class="taxon"><span class="code">T.5</span><span class="taxon-name">Heading</span><span class="badge">canonical</span><span class="taxon-gloss">of a section, in place</span></span></li> </ul> </li> <li><span class="taxon"><span class="code">W</span><a href="#card-work"><span class="taxon-name">Work</span></a><span class="taxon-gloss">authored, titled, canonized — any medium</span></span> <ul> <li><span class="taxon"><span class="code">W.1</span><a href="#card-writing"><span class="taxon-name">Writing</span></a><span class="taxon-gloss">a work whose medium is language</span></span> <ul> <li><span class="taxon"><span class="code">W.2</span><a href="#card-document"><span class="taxon-name">Document</span></a><span class="taxon-gloss">the first catalogued form</span></span> <ul> <li><span class="taxon"><span class="code">W.2a</span><span class="taxon-name">Essay</span><span class="taxon-gloss">its synopsis is a thesis</span></span></li> <li><span class="taxon"><span class="code">W.2b</span><span class="taxon-name">Article</span><span class="taxon-gloss">document within a reference work</span></span></li> <li><span class="taxon"><span class="code">W.2c</span><a href="#card-chapter"><span class="taxon-name">Chapter</span></a><span class="taxon-gloss">a document that belongs to a book</span></span></li> </ul> </li> <li><span class="taxon"><span class="code">W.3</span><a href="#card-book"><span class="taxon-name">Book</span></a><span class="badge role">cover</span><span class="taxon-gloss">the catalogue becomes an organ</span></span> <ul> <li><span class="taxon"><span class="code">W.3a</span><a href="#card-anthology"><span class="taxon-name">Anthology</span></a><span class="taxon-gloss">authorship moves into the entries</span></span></li> <li><span class="taxon"><span class="code">W.3b</span><a href="#card-textbook"><span class="taxon-name">Textbook</span></a><span class="taxon-gloss">specified by a subject</span></span></li> <li><span class="taxon"><span class="code">W.3c</span><a href="#card-biography"><span class="taxon-name">Biography</span></a><span class="taxon-gloss">subject-of is a person</span></span> <ul> <li><span class="taxon"><span class="code">W.3d</span><a href="#card-autobiography"><span class="taxon-name">Autobiography</span></a><span class="taxon-gloss">author == subject-of</span></span></li> <li><span class="taxon"><span class="code">W.3e</span><span class="taxon-name">Canonical biography</span><span class="badge">canonical</span><span class="taxon-gloss">of a subject — a whole book</span></span></li> </ul> </li> <li><span class="taxon"><span class="code">W.4</span><a href="#card-referencework"><span class="taxon-name">Reference work</span></a><span class="taxon-gloss">a book whose body is a catalogue</span></span> <ul> <li><span class="taxon"><span class="code">W.4a</span><a href="#card-dictionary"><span class="taxon-name">Dictionary</span></a><span class="taxon-gloss">entries contain definitions</span></span></li> <li><span class="taxon"><span class="code">W.4b</span><a href="#card-encyclopedia"><span class="taxon-name">Encyclopedia</span></a><span class="taxon-gloss">entries contain articles</span></span></li> <li><span class="taxon"><span class="code">W.5</span><a href="#card-subject"><span class="taxon-name">Subject</span></a><span class="taxon-gloss">entries point at whole books</span></span> <ul> <li><span class="taxon"><span class="code">W.6</span><a href="#card-librarycatalogue"><span class="taxon-name">Library catalogue</span></a><span class="taxon-gloss">the fixed point</span></span></li> </ul> </li> </ul> </li> </ul> </li> </ul> </li> </ul> </li> </ul> </div> <p class="note">Chapter descends from Document with almost no structural change — the difference is habitat, which is why containment stays a distinct relation from inheritance. Note Document does <em>not</em> descend from Section: the step from Text to Writing is a change of root — see § 3.</p> </section> <section id="event"> <div class="eyebrow">§ 3 · The Canonization Event</div> <h2>How new forms are born</h2> <p>Every major transition is the same event at successive rungs:</p> <div class="formal">1. content overflows its container 2. the parts acquire canonicals — headings, titles, synopses 3. a catalogue crystallizes over them — canonicals coupled to references 4. the container becomes a catalogue of those canonicals 5. the parts become first-class — nameable, addressable, authored</div> <p><strong>The Text/Work boundary.</strong> The first canonization event is visible in the skeleton as a change of root: a Document does not extend Section. Text becomes a writing not by growing longer but by acquiring title, author, and synopsis — the domain's own distinction between text and work, drawn as a class boundary. Authorship becomes expressible exactly here, because here for the first time there is a catalogue to sign; below it, parts inherit the author of their nearest catalogued ancestor.</p> <p><strong>Page, excluded on principle.</strong> A page is an artifact of how a book is viewed — the same text flows into different pages on different readers. Page belongs to the runtime trace, not the catalogue.</p> </section> <section id="ladders"> <div class="eyebrow">§ 4 · The Two Ladders</div> <h2>Analogies that repeat at every rung</h2> <h3>The compression ladder</h3> <p>Every rung has its canonical compression — and each is built from text of a <em>lower</em> rung than what it stands for. The catalogue at rung <em>n</em> is populated from rung <em>n−k</em>.</p> <table class="ladder"> <tr><th>Stands for</th><th>Canonical</th><th>Built at the scale of</th></tr> <tr><td>word</td><td class="c">definition</td><td>a sentence</td></tr> <tr><td>paragraph</td><td class="c">topic sentence</td><td>a sentence</td></tr> <tr><td>section</td><td class="c">heading</td><td>a phrase</td></tr> <tr><td>document</td><td class="c">title + synopsis</td><td>a name · a paragraph</td></tr> <tr><td>book</td><td class="c">blurb · spine</td><td>a paragraph · a title</td></tr> <tr><td>subject</td><td class="c">scope note · canonical biography</td><td>a paragraph · a whole book</td></tr> </table> <p class="note">The synopsis is load-bearing: because every document has one, any set of documents has an <em>emergent</em> catalogue. Without canonicals, catalogues would have to be authored from nothing; with them, cataloguing is gathering.</p> <h3>The overflow law</h3> <p>An entry holds a canonical plus a payload — until the payload outgrows the entry, and containment turns into reference. The reference works, ordered by payload, walk straight through the threshold:</p> <table class="ladder"> <tr><th>Reference work</th><th>Entry canonical</th><th>Entry payload</th></tr> <tr><td>dictionary</td><td class="c">the word itself</td><td>a definition — contained</td></tr> <tr><td>encyclopedia</td><td class="c">a title</td><td>an article — contained, straining</td></tr> <tr><td>subject</td><td class="c">a synopsis</td><td><strong>none — a link to a whole book</strong></td></tr> </table> <p>This is the identical event as document → book → subject on the composition spine: one law, seen twice.</p> </section> <section id="cards"> <div class="eyebrow">§ 5 · The Cards</div> <h2>One card per form</h2> <p>Properties in the grey band mirror the skeleton exactly. <em>Extends</em> is inheritance; braces are composition.</p> <div class="runghead">Root · Name</div> <div class="card" id="card-name"> <div class="card-head"><span class="code">N</span><span class="taxon-name">Name</span><span class="lineage">extends $Chemical</span></div> <div class="card-body"> <div class="props">{ target }</div> <p>The pure pointing thing: one property, what it is about. Contributes no content to what carries it. Her name is the founding example.</p> </div> </div> <div class="card" id="card-reference"> <div class="card-head"><span class="code">N.1</span><span class="taxon-name">Reference</span><span class="lineage">extends Name</span></div> <div class="card-body"> <div class="props">+ { site: Text }</div> <p>A name anchored at a site: pointing in use. Modeled as inheritance — a reference is a name with strictly more structure. The rival reading is composition, a pairing (name, site). The skeleton compiles under the first; Doug adjudicates the semantics (Q.3).</p> </div> </div> <div class="card" id="card-link"> <div class="card-head"><span class="code">N.2</span><span class="taxon-name">Link</span><span class="lineage">extends Reference</span></div> <div class="card-body"> <div class="props">+ { traverse() }</div> <p>A reference made traversable: following it is an act in the library's runtime. Links are how reading moves.</p> </div> </div> <div class="card" id="card-signature"> <div class="card-head"><span class="code">N.3</span><span class="taxon-name">Signature</span><span class="lineage">extends Link</span></div> <div class="card-body"> <div class="props">target: Autobiography · site: Writing</div> <p>Both ends constrained: from an authored work to its author's canonical autobiography — the most global representation of her narrative. The residue the between-the-lines act leaves inside the closure: the signature is in the book; the signing is not.</p> </div> </div> <div class="card" id="card-title"> <div class="card-head"><span class="code">N.4</span><span class="taxon-name">Title</span><span class="lineage">extends Name · canonical of Work</span></div> <div class="card-body"> <div class="props">+ { of: Work }</div> <p>The intersection: a name that is also a designated stand-in. Dual citizenship is exactly what lets a catalogue entry be one small thing. Historical note: the title evolved from the <em>incipit</em> — before titles, catalogues identified works by their opening words. The stemma of the title is documented history.</p> </div> </div> <div class="runghead">Root · Text</div> <div class="card" id="card-text"> <div class="card-head"><span class="code">T</span><span class="taxon-name">Text</span><span class="lineage">extends $Chemical</span></div> <div class="card-body"> <div class="props">{ }</div> <p><em>Textus</em>, the woven thing: language content as such — before authorship, before canonization, before address.</p> </div> </div> <div class="card" id="card-word"> <div class="card-head"><span class="code">T.1</span><span class="taxon-name">Word</span><span class="lineage">extends Text</span></div> <div class="card-body"> <div class="props">{ spelling }</div> <p>The atomic content-bearing unit. Whether the floor lies here or descends to the reference itself is Q.4.</p> </div> </div> <div class="card" id="card-sentence"> <div class="card-head"><span class="code">T.2</span><span class="taxon-name">Sentence</span><span class="lineage">extends Text</span></div> <div class="card-body"> <div class="props">{ words* }</div> <p>The first complete relational form — the linguistic body of <span class="mono">x =t&gt; y</span>. The first true emergence: words alone assert nothing; a sentence asserts. Smallest text that can be true or false.</p> </div> </div> <div class="card" id="card-paragraph"> <div class="card-head"><span class="code">T.3</span><span class="taxon-name">Paragraph</span><span class="lineage">extends Text</span></div> <div class="card-body"> <div class="props">{ sentences* }</div> <p>Sentences gathered on one focus. First form with a topic — carried in the weave, not yet written above it.</p> </div> </div> <div class="card" id="card-section"> <div class="card-head"><span class="code">T.4</span><span class="taxon-name">Section</span><span class="lineage">extends Text</span></div> <div class="card-body"> <div class="props">{ paragraphs*, heading }</div> <p>Paragraphs under a heading: the topic acquires its canonical, written in place. The first rung where canonization happens inside a form, before any catalogue exists to collect it.</p> </div> </div> <div class="card" id="card-synopsis"> <div class="card-head"><span class="code">T.3a</span><span class="taxon-name">Synopsis</span><span class="lineage">extends Paragraph · canonical · derivative</span></div> <div class="card-body"> <div class="props">+ { of: Document, derivedFrom: Document }</div> <p>Ordinary paragraph-scale text, derived from its original, designated the standing stand-in. Draft 1 made compressions a third lineage; the vocabulary refused to name it, and it was right — a compression is text playing the canon's part. Near relatives: summary (undesignated), abstract (the essay's synopsis), digest, abridgment, epitome — the classical genre that is literally the cut-down of a larger work.</p> </div> </div> <div class="runghead">Root · Work</div> <div class="card" id="card-work"> <div class="card-head"><span class="code">W</span><span class="taxon-name">Work</span><span class="lineage">extends $Chemical</span></div> <div class="card-body"> <div class="props">{ title, author: Signature, content: unknown }</div> <p>The authored, titled, canonized unit — medium unspecified. The genus is real: the library already holds the atlas (a book of maps), the art book (of plates), the score (of music). Ana's whole shelf lives here. Whether this root can be grounded lower is Q.6.</p> </div> </div> <div class="card" id="card-writing"> <div class="card-head"><span class="code">W.1</span><span class="taxon-name">Writing</span><span class="lineage">extends Work</span></div> <div class="card-body"> <div class="props">content: Text</div> <p>The language species of work: a writing is not merely about the representation of language — it is the representation of language, definitionally. "Her writings"; "a writing," the law's own term; holy writ. Our entire spine from here up consists of writings.</p> </div> </div> <div class="card" id="card-document"> <div class="card-head"><span class="code">W.2</span><span class="taxon-name">Document</span><span class="lineage">extends Writing</span></div> <div class="card-body"> <div class="props">+ { sections*, synopsis }</div> <p>The first catalogued form — canonization event № 1. The whole acquires title and canonical synopsis; sections have headings; the form takes on catalogue structure relative to its own content. Addressable from outside by its own canonicals. First place there is a catalogue to sign.</p> </div> </div> <div class="card" id="card-chapter"> <div class="card-head"><span class="code">W.2c</span><span class="taxon-name">Chapter</span><span class="lineage">extends Document</span></div> <div class="card-body"> <div class="props">+ { belongsTo: Book }</div> <p>A document that belongs to a book: the difference is habitat, not structure. Chapters can gather into parts — an intermediate organ, not a genre. Standing proof that containment and inheritance are distinct relations.</p> </div> </div> <div class="card" id="card-book"> <div class="card-head"><span class="code">W.3</span><span class="taxon-name">Book</span><span class="lineage">extends Writing · implements Cover</span></div> <div class="card-body"> <div class="props">{ chapters*, contents*, subjectOf } · Cover = { title, author, subjectOf, contents }</div> <p>Canonization event № 2: the catalogue separates into a distinct organ, the Cover — the shell of the book, holding title, author's signature, subject-of, and the table of contents. The universal medium of the ELM: anything specifiable can be given a book, which is why the library can close under them. Cover is modeled as a role the book implements, deferring Q.2 while staying compilable.</p> </div> </div> <div class="card" id="card-anthology"> <div class="card-head"><span class="code">W.3a</span><span class="taxon-name">Anthology</span><span class="lineage">extends Book</span></div> <div class="card-body"> <div class="props">entries: + { author: Signature } each</div> <p>Chapters by many authors. Authorship stops being a single fact of the cover and becomes a column of the table of contents; the cover's author is demoted to curator. Legal under inheritance-carries-truth: the fact of authorship is inherited, its location respecified.</p> </div> </div> <div class="card" id="card-textbook"> <div class="card-head"><span class="code">W.3b</span><span class="taxon-name">Textbook</span><span class="lineage">extends Book</span></div> <div class="card-body"> <div class="props">+ { specifiedBy: Subject }</div> <p>A book that exists to satisfy a specification a subject writes and enforces — the first genre defined by an obligation. A subject may require that a book of this type exist within it in order to be that subject.</p> </div> </div> <div class="card" id="card-biography"> <div class="card-head"><span class="code">W.3c</span><span class="taxon-name">Biography</span><span class="lineage">extends Book</span></div> <div class="card-body"> <div class="props">subjectOf → a person's canonical</div> <p>A book whose subject-of dereferences to a person. It does not contain its person; it is the reference network a reader traverses to encounter them.</p> </div> </div> <div class="card" id="card-autobiography"> <div class="card-head"><span class="code">W.3d</span><span class="taxon-name">Autobiography</span><span class="lineage">extends Biography</span></div> <div class="card-body"> <div class="props">author == subjectOf</div> <p>The strange loop written as a cover: the genre the whole stemma exists to make possible. The author's stand-in inside the closure; the far end of every signature; in Eirian's library, the book that closes the loop with the master catalogue.</p> </div> </div> <div class="card" id="card-referencework"> <div class="card-head"><span class="code">W.4</span><span class="taxon-name">Reference work</span><span class="lineage">extends Book · implements Catalogue</span></div> <div class="card-body"> <div class="props">+ { entries* } — distinct from the cover's contents*</div> <p>A book whose body is a catalogue over some population: dictionary over words, thesaurus over word-relations, encyclopedia over referents, atlas over places, bibliography over cited books. The genus dictionary and encyclopedia anchor — and the compiler's discovery lives here: a reference work carries <em>two</em> catalogues, its cover's contents and its body's entries. Draft 2a conflated them; the type-checker refused.</p> </div> </div> <div class="card" id="card-dictionary"> <div class="card-head"><span class="code">W.4a</span><span class="taxon-name">Dictionary</span><span class="lineage">extends Reference work</span></div> <div class="card-body"> <div class="props">entries: { canonical: Word, payload: Definition }</div> <p>Catalogue over words; each entry contains its payload, a definition — the sentence-scale canonical of a word's meaning. Below the overflow threshold.</p> </div> </div> <div class="card" id="card-encyclopedia"> <div class="card-head"><span class="code">W.4b</span><span class="taxon-name">Encyclopedia</span><span class="lineage">extends Reference work</span></div> <div class="card-body"> <div class="props">entries: { canonical: Title, payload: Article }</div> <p>Catalogue over referents; each entry contains a whole article — a document. Containment straining: one rung before the overflow.</p> </div> </div> <div class="card" id="card-subject"> <div class="card-head"><span class="code">W.5</span><span class="taxon-name">Subject</span><span class="lineage">extends Reference work</span></div> <div class="card-body"> <div class="props">entries: { canonical: Synopsis, link: Link } · { specifications* }</div> <p>Canonization event № 3, and the overflow law crossing its threshold: entries stop containing and start pointing at whole books. Double-faced: functions <em>in</em> a catalogue (an entry that gets you to a region of the library) and serves <em>as</em> one (indexing the books once you arrive). A subject is a type of book — captured exactly, because the whole reference-work genus is. And the two senses of "subject" resolve here: books share a subject-of value; the population that role induces, canonicalized and labeled, <em>is</em> this genre. The species is the reification of the role.</p> </div> </div> <div class="card" id="card-librarycatalogue"> <div class="card-head"><span class="code">W.6</span><span class="taxon-name">Library catalogue</span><span class="lineage">extends Subject</span></div> <div class="card-body"> <div class="props">catalogues the library, including itself</div> <p>The fixed point of the recursion: the subject of all subjects, itself a book in the library it catalogues. Out of scope today; stubbed for shape.</p> </div> </div> </section> <section id="questions"> <div class="eyebrow">§ 6 · Open Parameters</div> <h2>Questions held for Doug</h2> <div class="question"><span class="q-code">Q.1</span><h4>Is derivation a relation, or history?</h4> <p>The canonization event resembles transduction at book scale. If so, the stemma is diachronic history and existing machinery covers it. The skeleton keeps Derivative as an explicit interface so the question stays visible.</p></div> <div class="question"><span class="q-code">Q.2</span><h4>Is Cover a genre or a role?</h4> <p>Modeled as an interface the book implements — the role reading — with the genre reading still available. The compiler is content either way; the semantics may not be.</p></div> <div class="question"><span class="q-code">Q.3</span><h4>Does Reference extend Name?</h4> <p>Inheritance reading: a reference is a name plus a site — strictly more structure, honoring the correction that started Draft 2. Composition reading: a reference is a pairing (name, site). The skeleton compiles under the first; the pairing reading is nearer to (x,y).</p></div> <div class="question"><span class="q-code">Q.4</span><h4>Where is the floor?</h4> <p>Word, or all the way down to reference — her name close to a single symbol, a reference nearly (x,y). If the floor is reference, the roots touch the primitive directly and the mapping onto code goes all the way down.</p></div> <div class="question"><span class="q-code">Q.5</span><h4>Can Work be grounded lower?</h4> <p>Three roots stand: Name, Text, Work. Work resists grounding in Text (a work of art has no text). Either the genus is genuinely a third root, or there is a deeper root beneath all three that we have not named yet.</p></div> <div class="question"><span class="q-code">Q.6</span><h4>Which near-relatives are genres?</h4> <p>Summary, abstract, digest, abridgment, epitome — currently relatives of Synopsis, undesignated or specialized. Journal, manual, treatise — candidate genres of Book not yet carded. Cheap to add; expensive to add wrongly.</p></div> </section> <footer> <span>SRT · The Semantics of Books · Draft 2</span> <span>plate + skeleton · a Dewey decimal for the whole library</span> </footer> </div> </body> </html> HTMLEOF echo "written: $(wc -l < /mnt/user-data/outputs/srt_semantics_of_books.html) lines"
Presented files
Both documents are up, and your prediction about the two views came true on the very first compile. The type-checker rejected my hierarchy — and it was a semantic catch, not a syntactic one: I had a reference work's body entries overriding the book's cover contents as one property. The compiler refused, and the refusal was correct — a dictionary carries two catalogues: its cover's table of contents (a book's catalogue, over chapters) and its body (a catalogue over words). Draft 2 records the discovery on the ReferenceWork card with credit to the compiler. After the fix: clean under --strict.
How the skeleton is organized, primitive → derived:
The relational grammar is now explicit and fourfold: extends = inheritance (adds structure within a rung) · properties = composition (ascends rungs) · implements = designation (roles conferred) · Derivative = stemmatic descent, kept deliberately separate so Q.1 stays visible.
Three roots off 0: Name, Text, Work — with a new open parameter (Q.5) because Work resisted grounding in Text, and I'd rather record the resistance than force it.
The dissolved K-column became the code's prettiest pattern: Canonical<Of> as an interface, so every rung of the compression ladder is ordinary text plus a designation —Definition extends Sentence implements Canonical<Word>, Synopsis extends Paragraph implements Canonical<Document>, Derivative<Document>, up to CanonicalBiography extends Biography implements Canonical<Subject> — the ladder's top rung, where the stand-in is a whole book, typed exactly as Eirian's canonical-biography field.
The overflow law is typed: CatalogueEntry<C,P> holds canonical + payload? | link? — dictionary contains definitions, encyclopedia contains articles, and Subject's entries drop the payload for a mandatory link. Containment turning into reference, visible in the signature.
The text/work boundary is a change of root: Document extends Writing, not Section — the canonization event drawn as a class boundary, which I think is the skeleton's most honest sentence.
Cover is an interface Book implements, deferring Q.2 while compiling.
