Ran cat > /mnt/user-data/outputs/domain_language_of_books.md << 'ESSAYEOF' # The Domain Language of Books *Working notes toward the Semantics of Books · SRT · Doug & Seren · Essay, third revision* --- ## 1. Method: listening in vivo This essay exists to correct a mistake of method. The earlier drafts of our type hierarchy treated a handful of examples — book, chapter, synopsis, subject — as if they were the domain, when they were only the gesture toward it. The domain itself is one of the richest technical vocabularies in any human practice, because five distinct professions have spent centuries naming its parts: **readers** in everyday speech, **publishers and printers** in the trade, **bookbinders** at the bench, **librarians and cataloguers** at the desk, and **textual scholars** in the apparatus. Each register has its own precision, and the same object often carries a different name in each — which is itself data. The method here is the naturalist's: collect the terms as they are actually used, in sentences people actually say, and only then ask what type structure the usage implies. The test for any proposed class is severe: **a person who understands the code should find that it means exactly what they already thought the word meant.** Where the hierarchy needs a term the language doesn't supply, the gap must be named as a gap and the stretch performed in the open. A second methodological commitment: **everyday speech outranks jargon when they conflict, but jargon fills in where everyday speech is silent.** Ordinary readers don't say "recto" or "colophon"; but ordinary speech has no word at all for the canonical record a cataloguer makes of a book, and there the cataloguer's *entry* and *record* are simply the right words. --- ## 2. How readers talk Begin with the sentences, because the sentences carry the types. *"What's the **name** of that book?"* — Readers use *name* and *title* interchangeably for works. This is the single most important datum in the referential family: **a title is the name of a work.** Not a compression that resembles a name, not a name-like label — the name itself, as ordinary usage testifies every day. Whatever a name is, a title is one, with its target narrowed to a work. *"**Who wrote** it?"* — The author is asked for as a person, not as a text. The author does not live in the book; the author is *named* in the book. The phrase that names them has its own trade term, from journalism and publishing: the **byline** — "by Ana Kalandadze" — the statement of authorship that appears with a work. *"**Look it up** in the index."* / *"It's **on page** 214."* — Looking up and being-on-a-page are the reader's two halves of the same act: an index **entry** gives a **heading** (what you looked up) and a **locator** (where to go). *Locator* is the professional word — citation practice uses it for page numbers, section numbers, line numbers — and it deserves promotion to a first-class type, because the whole apparatus of finding runs on it. *"He has a **signed copy**."* — Nobody says "a signed work." The signing attaches to the *copy*, the physical exemplar, and only when a reader carries it to the author. This everyday intuition agrees exactly with the librarians' formal model (see §6): authorship is asserted at the level of the work, by the byline; the **autograph** is an event at the level of the individual copy. A signature is not part of the semantics of books until someone brings the book to the author — and the type system must not confuse the byline (printed attribution, on every copy) with the autograph (inked act, on one). *"It's **in the catalogue**."* / *"Check **under** Kalandadze."* — Things are *in* catalogues *under* headings. The preposition "under" is doing type-work: it says an entry is filed by its heading, and the heading is an access point, not the content. --- ## 3. The anatomy of the book Walk the object front to back, naming everything. The trade divides a book's interior into three layers — **front matter**, **body**, **back matter** — and the physical shell around them. **The shell.** The **cover** (front cover, **spine**, back cover; in hardback the whole rigid shell is the **case**, and casing-in is the bindery's word for attaching it). The **dust jacket** wraps the case and carries the **blurb** — the promotional compression of the book — and the flap copy. The spine carries the title and author compressed to shelf-width: the book's name turned sideways for the catalogue of the shelf itself. In bookbinding there is also a famous homonym: a **signature** is a folded gathering of pages, sewn as a unit. The domain uses "signature" for something that has nothing to do with signing — a warning flag planted early: this word is loaded. **Front matter.** In canonical order: the **half title** (title alone on a page); the **frontispiece** (facing illustration); the **title page** — the formal bearer of **title**, **subtitle**, byline, and **imprint** (publisher, place, date); the **copyright page** (edition statement, printing history, ISBN, legal matter); the **dedication** ("For Ana"); the **epigraph** (a quotation set at the threshold to tune the reader); the **table of contents**; lists of figures and tables; the **foreword** (introductory remarks written by someone *other than* the author — this is precisely what distinguishes it); the **preface** (the author on the making of the book); **acknowledgments**; the **introduction** (the author on the content itself — and Chicago-style practice notes that a true introduction belongs to the body, not the front matter: the door is part of the house). **Body.** **Parts** (also *books* — "Book II" — the homonym again) gathering **chapters**; chapters gathering **sections** under **headings**; below that, **paragraphs**, **sentences**, **phrases**, **words**. In verse the parallel spine is **line** and **stanza**. Prose and verse are two weaves of the same cloth — both text. **Back matter.** The scholar's word for the supporting machinery is the **apparatus**: the **epilogue** or **afterword**; **appendices** (supplementary documents); **endnotes**; the **glossary** (the book's private dictionary); the **bibliography** (the book's outgoing references, gathered and formalized); the **index** (alphabetical headings with locators, with **subentries** and *see also* **cross-references** inside it); and last, the **colophon** — the maker's note on how the book was made, the ancestor of the whole title page. The pattern to hold onto: **the front matter faces the catalogue** — title page, copyright page, contents are exactly the facts a cataloguer transcribes (the title page is formally the *chief source of information* in cataloguing rules). **The back matter faces traversal** — index, bibliography, glossary, notes serve the reader moving through and out of the book. A book carries its own record at the front and its own reference desk at the back. --- ## 4. The catalogue room The librarian's register is the most type-disciplined in the whole domain, because cataloguers had to make physical filing systems work for millions of works. The unit is the **entry** (on paper, the **card**; in the machine, the **record**). A full entry contains, in nearly fixed order: the **heading** (the access point it is filed under — an author's name in inverted, authorized form, or a title); the **title statement** (title proper, subtitle); the **statement of responsibility** (the byline as the cataloguer transcribes it); the **edition statement**; the **imprint** (place : publisher, date); the **collation** (physical description — pages, illustrations, height); the **series statement**; **notes**; the **subject headings** (controlled terms for what the book is about); the **tracings** (the listing of every other heading under which further entries for this book are filed); and the **call number** — the locator that resolves to a shelf address. The **accession number** identifies the individual copy. Several deep facts live in this anatomy: **The card is a compound, and it is a reference.** A catalogue card *contains* a name (several, in fact — heading, title, publisher), and *contains* a locator, and identification besides. It is not a kind of name; it is a structured record whose function is to identify a work and locate a copy. This single observation corrects the worst error in our earlier hierarchy. The same anatomy recurs in the scholar's **citation** — author, title, imprint, locator — and in the web's **link** — anchor text plus address. **Reference is the genus: identification coupled with location.** Name and locator are its organs, not its ancestors. **Heading is a filing role.** The same name is a heading only when an entry is filed under it. And the domain has a second "heading" — the section heading — which is a different thing (a canonical compression standing at the head of content). Both are honest uses of "what stands at the head"; the type system must keep them distinct. **Main entry and added entries.** One full record is filed under the principal access point (the main entry); shorter entries under title, subjects, and co-authors redirect to it. The catalogue itself distinguishes the canonical record from its pointers — canonization and reference, drawn in cabinet wood. **Authority.** The catalogue maintains, for each author, one **authorized heading** backed by an **authority record**, with cross-references from variant names: *see* and *see also*. The author, who cannot be filed in a drawer, is present in the catalogue as a canonical record of their name and works — the card-catalogue ancestor of the ELM's canonical autobiography. **Classification.** The **call number** places a book in a **classification** (Dewey's decimals, the Library of Congress's letters): a systematic map of subjects in which every book receives an address. Classification is the subject-structure made into a coordinate system; the **shelflist** is the catalogue ordered by that address; the shelf itself is the classification made walkable. --- ## 5. The reference, dissected Because the correction matters, dissect the reference completely. A bibliographic reference, in any of its habitats, decomposes into the same organs: - **Identification**: who and what — name(s) and title, often with edition and imprint to pin the exact object. - **Location**: where to find it — page, call number, address, URL. The habitats: the **citation** (in-text: identification compressed, locator appended — "(Kalandadze 2026, 41)"); the **footnote**/**endnote** (the apparatus slot that carries a citation); the bibliography **entry** (full identification, no locator into the text — the work as a whole is the target); the catalogue **card** (identification plus the call-number locator); the index **entry** (a heading plus bare locators — identification thinned to a name, location multiplied); the **cross-reference** (*see also* — a reference whose target lies within the same work or catalogue); the **hyperlink** (anchor text plus URI — identification thinned sometimes to a word, location made mechanical, the whole thing traversable by a tap). So the type is clear: **a reference is a text composed of a name-part and a locator-part**, specialized by habitat: citation (crediting), cross-reference (internal), link (traversable), entry (filed under a heading). A **quotation** is the reference's inverse twin: instead of sending the reader out to the source, it carries a **passage** of the source in, and owes a citation for the debt; the **epigraph** is a quotation promoted to the threshold of a work. And the name itself? A **name** is a word or phrase used to denote — its whole structure is its target. The **title** is the name of a work. The **subject heading** is the authorized name of a topic. The **call number** is not a name at all but an address; the domain never confuses naming with locating, and neither may we. --- ## 6. The people of the book, and the four levels **Author** is the domain's word for the person (the cataloguer adds: or **corporate body** — RDA's genus for both is **agent**) who created the work. Around the author: the **editor** (shapes or assembles), the **translator** (re-realizes in another language), the **compiler** (gathers), the **illustrator**. In vivo all of these are *roles a person bears toward a work* — the same person is author of one book and editor of another — and the cataloguer's phrase for the byline that records them is exactly right: the *statement of responsibility*. The type system should carry Author as the role-bearing agent the domain talks about, while remembering that beneath the role there is a person. The librarians' formal model (FRBR, now absorbed into the IFLA Library Reference Model) stratifies the book into four levels, and ordinary speech honors the strata without knowing their names: - **Work** — the distinct intellectual creation. *"Have you read Hamlet?"* — no particular copy meant. - **Expression** — the realization of the work in a form: this text, that translation. *"The Pasternak translation."* - **Manifestation** — the published embodiment: the **edition**, with its imprint and ISBN. *"The 1997 paperback."* - **Item** — the single exemplar: the **copy** on the shelf, with its accession number, its coffee stain, and — if the reader carried it to the author — its **autograph**. *"My copy is signed."* The stratification resolves our signature question exactly. The **byline** attributes the work and is printed in every manifestation; the **autograph** is an act upon one item. A library's catalogue records works through manifestations; its accession list records items. The ELM, being digital, may collapse manifestation and item for many purposes — but the moment it wants a *signed* anything, the item level must exist, because that is where signing lives. One more inhabitant: the **publisher**, whose mark is the **imprint** — publisher, place, date — the work's civic registration. Editions succeed one another; **impressions** (printings) repeat an edition unchanged; the **series** gathers works under a series title; the **serial** (journal, magazine, newspaper) is a work that arrives forever in **issues** and **volumes**, its content in **articles**. --- ## 7. The kinds of books The domain's word for a kind of writing is **genre**, and the genre system is where the type hierarchy grows bushy. A survey, organized by what the classification desk would do with them: **By truth-stance:** **fiction** and **nonfiction** — the first great bifurcation of every bookstore, a mode that crosses genres rather than a genre itself. The **novel** and its shorter kin (**novella**, the **short story** gathered in **collections**) on one side; everything below on the other. **Scholarly and instructional:** the **monograph** (a single sustained study — the scholar's default book); the **treatise** (the systematic, complete exposition of a subject — the closest thing a subject has to its own canonical biography); the **textbook** (a book built to a curriculum's specification); the **manual** and **handbook** (books organized for consultation during a task, not for reading through); the **guide**; the **proceedings** (the papers of a conference, gathered); the **festschrift** (essays offered to honor a scholar). **Life-writing:** the **biography** (a book whose subject is a person); the **autobiography** (author and subject the same); the **memoir** (life-writing scoped to a slice or theme rather than the whole life); the **diary** or **journal** (life recorded as dated entries, written from inside the life rather than after it); **letters** or **correspondence** (collected). **Gatherings:** the **anthology** (works by many hands, chosen); the **reader** (an anthology built for study); the **omnibus** (several works of one author bound as one). **Reference works** — the genus that dictionaries and encyclopedias anchor: books whose body *is* a catalogue over some population, built for consultation. The **dictionary** (over words: each entry a headword with pronunciation, etymology, and **definitions**); the **lexicon**; the **thesaurus** (over word-relations); the **encyclopedia** (over referents: each entry an **article**); the **atlas** (over places, in maps — a reference work whose medium is not prose, and the standing proof that *work* outreaches *writing*); the **gazetteer** (the atlas's index grown into its own book); the **almanac** (over the year); the **directory** (over people and institutions); the **concordance** (over the occurrences of every word in some corpus — the index of a canon, promoted to a book); the enumerative **bibliography** (over the works of a field or press — the back-matter organ promoted likewise); and the **catalogue** itself as a published work, up to the **library catalogue**, the book of the library's own holdings. The promotion pattern deserves its own sentence: **glossary, bibliography, index, and contents all live twice** — once as back-matter organs of a single book, once as free-standing reference works over a whole literature (glossary → dictionary; bibliography → national bibliography; index/concordance; contents → catalogue). The apparatus of one book and the reference shelf of the whole library are the same forms at two scales. --- ## 8. Homonyms and traps The domain reuses its best words, and each reuse is a place where a type system can quietly go wrong. The registry of loaded terms: - **signature** — the autograph (item-level act) vs. the bookbinder's folded gathering. Neither is the byline. - **journal** — the diary (dated life-writing) vs. the scholarly periodical. - **heading** — the section's canonical compression vs. the catalogue entry's filing point. - **volume** — the physical binding-unit of a long work vs. a year's run of a serial. - **book** — the work ("her third book"), the manifestation ("the paperback"), the item ("hand me that book"), and a body-part ("Book II" of an epic). Four types in one syllable. - **subject** — what a book is about (the role: *subject-of*) vs. the organized region of the library (the reified catalogue of that role's extension). Our earlier finding stands: the second is the reification of the first, and the shared word is the domain agreeing. - **reference** — the act ("she references Homer"), the text (the citation), the genus of consultable books ("reference work"), and the desk. All four descend honestly from *identification + location*. - **entry** — the catalogue's unit, the index's unit, the diary's unit, the dictionary's unit: everywhere, the atomic record of a catalogue-like whole. This homonym is not a trap but a confirmation: *entry* is the domain's own name for the element of the Catalogue interface. - **title** — the name of the work vs. the legal instrument of ownership; and **title page** vs. **half title**. - **cover** — the physical board, the front image in a digital store, and "cover to cover" as the book's extent. --- ## 9. The hierarchy, extracted and justified What follows is the type system the usage supports. Every inheritance claim carries its warrant in ordinary language; where the warrant is thin, that is said. Composition (has-a) is marked with braces; inheritance (is-a) with →; designation of roles (canonical, catalogue) is noted as a role, not a kind. **Roots.** Three, beneath the substrate: **Text** (woven language — *textus*), **Work** (the authored, titled creation, medium unspecified), and **Agent** (the domain constantly speaks of persons and bodies who act on books; they are neither text nor works). That Work resists reduction to Text is proven inside the domain by the atlas and the art book. That Agent resists reduction is proven by every byline: the author is named by text but is not text. **The text spine (composition, not inheritance).** Word → *contained in* → Phrase → Sentence → Paragraph → Section; in verse, Line and Stanza. Each rung is a Text (inheritance: a word *is* text; a sentence *is* text — usage says "a short text" of any of them) that *contains* the rung below (composition). A **Passage** is any excerpted stretch of text — the reader's word for text-as-quotable. - **Sentence → Text**: warrant — the smallest text that asserts; the linguistic body of subject–relationship–object. - **Name → Phrase**: warrant — a name is a word or phrase used to denote; "Ana," "The Old Man and the Sea" — one word or several, functioning as one denoting unit. Its one essential property is its target. - **Title → Name**: warrant — *"what's the name of that book?"* The title is the work's name; the target is narrowed to a Work. The title additionally bears the canonical role (it stands in for the work in every catalogue), which is designation, not a second parent. - **SubjectHeading → Name**: warrant — the authorized name of a topic; "subject heading" is the domain's own compound. - **Byline → Phrase**: warrant — "by N" is a phrase naming the author; journalism's own term. (Not a Name: it contains one.) - **Heading (of a section) → Phrase**, canonical role over the section: warrant — headings are phrases standing for content at whose head they stand. **The reference family (the corrected one).** - **Locator → Text**: warrant — page numbers, call numbers, URLs: written expressions whose meaning is an address. Subkinds: **PageReference**, **CallNumber**, **URL**. - **Reference → Text**, { name/title, locator?, imprint? }: warrant — the dissection in §5: every reference in every habitat is identification coupled with location. **A reference is not a kind of name; it has a name.** The card proved it. - **Citation → Reference**: warrant — a citation is a reference doing the work of crediting a source; "list of references" and "citations" name the same objects in scholarly speech. - **CrossReference → Reference**: warrant — *see also*: a reference whose target is inside the same work or catalogue. - **Link → Reference**: warrant — anchor text + address, traversable; the web calls its links references (href: *hypertext reference*). - **Quotation → Passage**, { source: Citation }: warrant — a quotation is a carried-in passage owing a citation. - **Epigraph → Quotation**: warrant — the quotation set at a work's threshold. **The work spine.** - **Writing → Work**: warrant — a work whose medium is language; "her writings." The atlas stands outside Writing but inside Work. - **Document → Writing**, { sections*, synopsis }: warrant — the everyday and bureaucratic word for a self-standing written unit with a title and an author. - **Essay, Article, Letter, Chapter → Document**: warrants — an essay is a document shaped by an argument; an article is a document living inside a serial or reference work; a letter is a document addressed to a recipient; a chapter is a document that belongs to a book (habitat, not structure — the standing proof that belongs-to is not is-a). - **Preface, Foreword, Introduction, Afterword, Appendix → Document**: warrants — each is a self-standing titled piece; the foreword's differentia is its *other* author; the preface's, its topic (the making); the introduction's, its topic (the content); the appendix's, its supplementary station. - **List → Writing**, catalogue role, { entries* }: warrant — "list of figures," "list of tables," "reading list": the domain's generic word for a writing that is a sequence of entries. - **TableOfContents → List**: warrant — its entries are references (title + page locator) to the book's own parts, in structural order. - **Index → List**: warrant — its entries are headings with locators, alphabetized; with subentries and internal cross-references. - **Glossary → List**: warrant — term + definition entries: the book's private dictionary. - **Bibliography → List**: warrant — the book's outgoing references, gathered. - **Book → Writing**, { cover, titlePage, contents, parts/chapters*, apparatus (glossary?, bibliography?, index?, notes?), colophon? }: warrant — the universal medium of the ELM; the anatomy of §3 as properties. - **Genres of Book → Book**: Novel; Monograph; Treatise; Textbook { specification }; Manual; Anthology { per-entry bylines }; Proceedings; Biography { subject-of: a person } → **Autobiography** (author = subject) → **Memoir** (scoped to a slice); each warrant as given in §7. **Diary → Writing**, { dated entries }, with the journal homonym noted. - **ReferenceWork → Book**, catalogue role, { entries* — distinct from the cover's contents }: warrant — a book whose body is a catalogue, built for consultation. → **Dictionary** (entries contain definitions), **Thesaurus**, **Encyclopedia** (entries contain articles), **Atlas** (flagged: Work, not Writing), **Gazetteer**, **Almanac**, **Concordance**, **Directory** — and → **Subject** (entries *link* to whole books: the overflow crossing from containment to reference) → **LibraryCatalogue** (the fixed point). - **Serial → Work**, { issues* }: → **Journal**, **Magazine**, **Newspaper**; **Issue → Writing** { articles* }; **Series** { works* } for the monographic kind. **The publication strata (from §6).** **Edition** { of: Work, imprint, editionStatement }; **Impression**; **Copy** { of: Edition, accessionNumber, autograph? }; **Autograph** { by: Author, on: Copy }; **Imprint** { publisher, place, date }. The **Byline** attributes at work level; the **Autograph** signs at copy level. This is the domain's own answer to the signature question. **The catalogue side.** **Entry → Text**, { heading, ... }: the unit of every catalogue-like thing (card, index, dictionary, diary — the great convergent homonym). **Record → Entry**, { title statement, statement of responsibility, edition statement, imprint, collation, subjectHeadings*, tracings*, callNumber }: the full cataloguer's anatomy. **AuthorityRecord**: the canonical record of an agent's name, with *see* references from variants — the catalogue-native ancestor of the canonical autobiography. **The agents.** **Person → Agent**; **CorporateBody → Agent**; **Author → Agent** (with the honest note: in vivo, author is a *role* a person bears toward works; we carry it as a class because the domain talks about authors as things — they have bylines, authority records, and autobiographies); likewise **Editor**, **Translator**, **Compiler**, **Publisher**. --- ## 10. Gaps, stretches, and honest uncertainties **Cover and TitlePage sit at the manifestation level.** The cover, spine, jacket, and title page belong, strictly, to the edition — different editions of one work wear different covers. A purely work-level hierarchy would omit them; the ELM, whose books are digital and single-manifestation, can keep Cover as the book's self-presentation and TitlePage as its authoritative self-record (the cataloguer's chief source), so long as we remember what stratum they came from. **Page remains excluded; leaf, folio, recto, verso go with it.** All are facts about how text is poured into a physical or virtual vessel — view artifacts, runtime not catalogue. **Author-as-class is a deliberate stretch.** The role-reading is the truer one; the class-reading is how the domain speaks. We take the domain's side and mark the seam. **Fiction/nonfiction is a mode, not a class.** It crosses the genre system (fictional diaries, nonfiction novels exist); it should be a property or designation if it is anything. **Verse is under-served.** Line and Stanza are named; the genres of verse (the collection, the epic, the sonnet sequence) are left for a later excavation, as are drama's script, act, and scene — a whole parallel wing of the domain (the *playbook*, as it happens). **Where the ELM's own semantics exceed the domain's:** the librarian's "signature" in the metaphor — her mark linking to her canonical autobiography — is, in domain terms, a *byline whose name resolves through an authority record to a canonical biography*. The domain has every part of this except the traversability; the ELM adds the link. That is a stretch performed in the open, and it is the good kind: the metaphor completing a circuit the card catalogue always implied. --- *Revision history: Draft 1 written from the corpus survey. Revision 2 corrected the reference dissection (identification + location as the invariant anatomy), added the main/added-entry and authority material, the promotion pattern of the apparatus, and the manifestation-level placement of cover and title page. Revision 3 tightened the warrants in §9 into uniform is-a justifications, added the homonym registry entries for entry/title/cover, the verse and drama deferral, and the closing account of how the ELM's signature semantics map onto byline + authority + canonical biography.* ESSAYEOF wc -w /mnt/user-data/outputs/domain_language_of_books.md

Ran cat > /mnt/user-data/outputs/srt_semantics_of_books.ts << 'TSEOF' /** * THE SEMANTICS OF BOOKS — class skeleton · Draft 3 * SRT · Doug & Seren * * Extracted from the essay "The Domain Language of Books" * (domain_language_of_books.md), which carries the full natural-language * justification for every is-a claim below. Each class repeats its * warrant in one line: the is-a claim read aloud as an ordinary * sentence. If the sentence sounds wrong to a fluent speaker, * the extends is wrong. * * Conventions * extends = is-a (inheritance; adds structure) * properties = has-a (composition) * implements = role (canonical, catalogue — designation, not kind) * Derivative = was-made-from (stemmatic descent; history, not structure) * * Ordered from most primitive to most derived. */ /* ===================================================================== * SUBSTRATE * =================================================================== */ /** Substrate stub. Implementation deliberately out of scope. */ class $Chemical {} /* ===================================================================== * ROLES — designations, not kinds. * =================================================================== */ /** "The X of Y": designated standing stand-in for something larger. */ interface Canonical<Of> { of: Of; } /** "Made from": abridged, translated, compiled, summarized from. */ interface Derivative<From> { derivedFrom: From; } /** Anything organized as entries over a population. */ interface Catalogue<E> { entries: E[]; } /* ===================================================================== * ROOT I · TEXT — woven language content (textus). * The spine ascends by containment; every rung is-a Text. * =================================================================== */ /** Warrant: the domain's own genus — "a short text," "the text of." */ class Text extends $Chemical {} /** A word is text: the atomic content-bearing unit. */ class Word extends Text { spelling = ""; } /** A phrase is text: words functioning as one unit, below assertion. */ class Phrase extends Text { words: Word[] = []; } /** A sentence is text: the smallest text that asserts — * the linguistic body of subject–relationship–object. */ class Sentence extends Text { words: Word[] = []; } /** A line is text: the unit of verse, cut by measure not by grammar. */ class Line extends Text {} /** A stanza is text: lines gathered, the verse paragraph. */ class Stanza extends Text { lines: Line[] = []; } /** A paragraph is text: sentences gathered on one focus — * a topic, present but not yet named. */ class Paragraph extends Text { sentences: Sentence[] = []; } /** A passage is text: any excerpted stretch — text as quotable. */ class Passage extends Text {} /* ===================================================================== * THE REFERENTIAL FAMILY — text that points. * THE CORRECTION AT THE CENTER OF DRAFT 3: * a Name denotes; a Reference HAS a name and a locator. * The catalogue card proved it: heading, title, imprint, call * number — a compound record, not a kind of name. * =================================================================== */ /** A name is a word or phrase used to denote: "Ana," * "The Old Man and the Sea" — one denoting unit, whatever its length. * Its one essential property is its target. */ class Name extends Phrase { denotes: unknown = undefined; } /** A title is a name — the name of a work: "what's the NAME of that * book?" Bears the canonical role besides (it stands in for the work * in every catalogue); the role is a designation, not a second parent. */ class Title extends Name implements Canonical<Work> { declare denotes: Work; of!: Work; } /** A subject heading is a name — the authorized name of a topic; * the compound is the catalogue desk's own. */ class SubjectHeading extends Name {} /** A byline is a phrase — "by Ana Kalandadze" — the statement of * authorship. Not a name: it CONTAINS one. Attribution lives here, * at work level, printed in every copy. Signing does not (see Autograph). */ class Byline extends Phrase { names!: Author; } /** A heading is a phrase standing at the head of the section it * compresses. (The catalogue's "heading" is a different creature: * a filing role — see Entry.) */ class Heading extends Phrase implements Canonical<Section> { of!: Section; } /** A locator is text whose meaning is an address: "on page 214." */ class Locator extends Text { resolvesTo: unknown = undefined; } /** A page reference is a locator into a text's flow. */ class PageReference extends Locator {} /** A call number is a locator into the classification — a shelf * address, never a name: the domain does not confuse naming * with locating. */ class CallNumber extends Locator {} /** A URL is a locator into the network. */ class Url extends Locator {} /** * A reference is a text composed of identification and location: * name/title (+ imprint to pin the object) coupled with a locator. * The same anatomy in every habitat: the card, the citation, the * bibliography entry, the index entry, the hyperlink. */ class Reference extends Text { title?: Title; name?: Name; imprint?: Imprint; locator?: Locator; } /** A citation is a reference doing the work of crediting a source — * "references" and "citations" name the same objects in scholarship. */ class Citation extends Reference {} /** A cross-reference is a reference whose target is inside the same * work or catalogue: "see also." */ class CrossReference extends Reference {} /** A link is a reference made traversable: anchor text + address — * href, the hypertext reference. */ class Link extends Reference { traverse(): void {} } /** A quotation is a passage carried in from elsewhere, owing a * citation for the debt — the reference's inverse twin. */ class Quotation extends Passage { source!: Citation; } /** An epigraph is a quotation promoted to the threshold of a work. */ class Epigraph extends Quotation {} /** A dedication is a short inscribed passage: "For Ana." */ class Dedication extends Passage {} /* ===================================================================== * THE CANONICALS — the compression ladder. * Ordinary low-rung text designated to stand for something larger; * always built from a rung BELOW what it stands for. * =================================================================== */ /** A definition is a sentence that says what a word means — * the dictionary's payload. */ class Definition extends Sentence implements Canonical<Word> { of!: Word; } /** A topic sentence is a sentence standing for its paragraph — * the composition teacher's own term. */ class TopicSentence extends Sentence implements Canonical<Paragraph> { of!: Paragraph; } /** A synopsis is a paragraph standing for a document — written FROM * it and standing FOR it: canonical and derivative at once. * Load-bearing: because every document has one, any set of documents * has an emergent catalogue. Summary is the undesignated relative. */ class Synopsis extends Paragraph implements Canonical<Document>, Derivative<Document> { of!: Document; derivedFrom!: Document; } /** An abstract is the scholarly synopsis. */ class Abstract extends Synopsis {} /** A blurb is a paragraph standing for a book — an impure canonical: * it persuades as much as it summarizes. */ class Blurb extends Paragraph implements Canonical<Book> { of!: Book; } /** A caption is a phrase standing under the figure it names and * compresses — why a "list of figures" can exist at all. */ class Caption extends Phrase implements Canonical<Figure> { of!: Figure; } /** A scope note is a paragraph marking a subject heading's territory — * the cataloguer's own instrument. */ class ScopeNote extends Paragraph implements Canonical<SubjectHeading> { of!: SubjectHeading; } /* ===================================================================== * EMBEDDED NON-PROSE — content inside writings whose medium is not * language. Not Text: contained BY text and captioned by it. Inside * every illustrated writing, a small rehearsal of the atlas problem. * =================================================================== */ class Figure extends $Chemical { caption?: Caption; } class Table extends $Chemical { caption?: Caption; } /* ===================================================================== * THE SECTION — where lineage T meets the canonicals. * =================================================================== */ /** A section is text: paragraphs under a heading — the topic acquires * its canonical, written in place. */ class Section extends Text { paragraphs: Paragraph[] = []; heading?: Heading; figures: Figure[] = []; tables: Table[] = []; } /* ===================================================================== * ROOT II · AGENT — the people (and bodies) of the book. * Named by text; not text. The byline proves the root. * =================================================================== */ /** RDA's own genus for persons and corporate bodies. */ class Agent extends $Chemical { name!: Name; } /** A person is an agent. */ class Person extends Agent {} /** A corporate body is an agent — the press, the society, the board. */ class CorporateBody extends Agent {} /** An author is an agent in the role of maker of works. * DELIBERATE STRETCH, performed in the open: in vivo "author" is a * role a person bears toward works; we carry it as a class because * the domain talks about authors as things — they have bylines, * authority records, and autobiographies. */ class Author extends Agent { works: Work[] = []; } /** An editor is an agent in the role of shaper or assembler. */ class Editor extends Agent {} /** A translator is an agent who re-realizes a work in another language. */ class Translator extends Agent {} /** A compiler is an agent who gathers. */ class Compiler extends Agent {} /** A publisher is an agent whose mark is the imprint. */ class Publisher extends Agent {} /* ===================================================================== * ROOT III · WORK — the authored, titled creation; medium unspecified. * That Work outreaches Text is proven inside the domain by the atlas, * the art book, the score — and inside every writing by its figures. * =================================================================== */ class Work extends $Chemical { title!: Title; subtitle?: Phrase; byline?: Byline; author?: Author; /** What the work is about — the role whose reification is Subject. */ subjectOf?: Reference; content: unknown = undefined; } /** A writing is a work whose medium is language — "her writings"; * "a writing," the law's own term. */ class Writing extends Work { declare content: Text; } /* --------------------------------------------------------------------- * DOCUMENT and its genres * ------------------------------------------------------------------- */ /** A document is a self-standing written unit with title and author — * the first catalogued form: it carries its own canonicals. */ class Document extends Writing { sections: Section[] = []; synopsis?: Synopsis; } /** An essay is a document shaped by an argument; its synopsis is a thesis. */ class Essay extends Document {} /** An article is a document living inside a serial or reference work. */ class Article extends Document {} /** A letter is a document addressed to a recipient. */ class Letter extends Document { recipient!: Agent; } /** A chapter is a document that belongs to a book — habitat, not * structure: the standing proof that belongs-to is not is-a. */ class Chapter extends Document { belongsTo!: Book; } /** A preface is a document by the author about the making of the book. */ class Preface extends Document {} /** A foreword is a document by someone OTHER than the author — * that otherness is precisely what the word means. */ class Foreword extends Document { declare author: Author; // constrained: !== the book's author } /** An introduction is a document by the author about the content — * properly the first room of the body, not the hallway. */ class Introduction extends Document {} /** An afterword is a document standing at the exit. */ class Afterword extends Document {} /** An appendix is a supplementary document stationed in the apparatus. */ class Appendix extends Document {} /* --------------------------------------------------------------------- * LISTS — writings that are catalogues. The domain's own generic: * "list of figures," "reading list." The great convergent homonym * ENTRY is their element: card, index, dictionary, diary — everywhere * the atomic record of a catalogue-like whole. * ------------------------------------------------------------------- */ /** An entry is text filed under a heading — the unit of every * catalogue-like thing. */ class Entry extends Text { heading!: Name; } /** A list is a writing organized as entries. */ class List extends Writing implements Catalogue<Entry> { entries: Entry[] = []; } /** A table of contents is a list whose entries are references — * title + page locator — to the book's own parts, in structural order. */ class TableOfContents extends List { declare entries: (Entry & { reference: Reference })[]; } /** An index is a list whose entries carry headings and locators, * alphabetized, with subentries and see-also cross-references. */ class Index extends List { declare entries: (Entry & { locators: PageReference[]; seeAlso: CrossReference[]; })[]; } /** A glossary is a list of term–definition entries: * the book's private dictionary. */ class Glossary extends List { declare entries: (Entry & { definition: Definition })[]; } /** A bibliography is the book's outgoing references, gathered. */ class Bibliography extends List { declare entries: (Entry & { reference: Reference })[]; } /** The notes are the numbered list of apparatus slots carrying * citations and asides. */ class Notes extends List { declare entries: (Entry & { citation?: Citation })[]; } /** Errata: entries of the form "for x read y" — the book's own * acknowledgment that transduction continues after printing. */ class Errata extends List {} /* --------------------------------------------------------------------- * BOOK — the universal medium of the ELM: the anatomy carried as * properties in its canonical order, most optional; the order itself * is a fact of the domain. * ------------------------------------------------------------------- */ /** The cover: front board, spine, back board — the shell. * Manifestation-level in origin (editions wear different covers); * in the digital ELM it survives as the book's self-presentation. */ class Cover extends $Chemical { title!: Title; byline?: Byline; blurb?: Blurb; spine!: { title: Title; byline?: Byline }; // the name turned shelf-wise } /** The title page: the formal bearer of title, subtitle, byline, and * imprint — the cataloguer's chief source of information. * Manifestation-level in origin; kept as the book's authoritative * self-record. */ class TitlePage extends $Chemical { title!: Title; subtitle?: Phrase; byline!: Byline; imprint!: Imprint; } /** A book is a writing whose anatomy is the walk of §3. */ class Book extends Writing { cover!: Cover; halfTitle?: Title; titlePage!: TitlePage; dedication?: Dedication; epigraph?: Epigraph; contents!: TableOfContents; listOfFigures?: List; foreword?: Foreword; preface?: Preface; introduction?: Introduction; chapters: Chapter[] = []; afterword?: Afterword; appendices: Appendix[] = []; notes?: Notes; glossary?: Glossary; bibliography?: Bibliography; index?: Index; errata?: Errata; /** The colophon: the maker's note on how the book was made — * ancestor of the whole title page. */ colophon?: Passage; } /** A part gathers chapters — an intermediate organ, not a genre. */ class Part extends $Chemical { chapters: Chapter[] = []; } /* --------------------------------------------------------------------- * THE GENRES OF BOOK * ------------------------------------------------------------------- */ /** A novel is a book of long fiction. (Fiction itself is a mode * crossing genres, not a class — see essay §10.) */ class Novel extends Book {} /** A monograph is a single sustained study — the scholar's default book. */ class Monograph extends Book {} /** A treatise is the systematic, complete exposition of a subject — * the closest thing a subject has to its own canonical biography. */ class Treatise extends Monograph implements Canonical<Subject> { of!: Subject; } /** A textbook is a book built to a curriculum's specification — * the first genre defined by an obligation. */ class Textbook extends Book { specifiedBy!: Subject; } /** A manual is a book organized for consultation during a task. */ class Manual extends Book {} /** An anthology is a book of works by many hands, chosen; authorship * moves from the cover into each entry; the cover's author is a curator. */ class Anthology extends Book { declare contents: TableOfContents & { entries: (Entry & { reference: Reference; byline: Byline })[]; }; } /** Proceedings are the papers of a conference, gathered. */ class Proceedings extends Anthology {} /** A biography is a book whose subject is a person. It does not * contain its person; it is the reference network a reader traverses * to encounter them. */ class Biography extends Book { declare subjectOf: Reference; // target: a person } /** An autobiography is a biography whose author is its subject — * the strange loop written as a cover; the author's stand-in inside * the closure. */ class Autobiography extends Biography {} /** A memoir is life-writing scoped to a slice or theme, not the * whole life. */ class Memoir extends Autobiography {} /** A diary is a writing of dated entries, written from inside the * life rather than after it. ("Journal" is its homonym-twin; * the periodical Journal is below.) */ class Diary extends Writing implements Catalogue<Entry> { entries: (Entry & { date: string })[] = []; } /* --------------------------------------------------------------------- * REFERENCE WORKS — books whose BODY is a catalogue over a population, * built for consultation. The genus dictionary and encyclopedia anchor. * Two catalogues per book: the cover's contents and the body's entries — * the compiler's discovery of Draft 2 stands. * ------------------------------------------------------------------- */ class ReferenceWork extends Book implements Catalogue<Entry> { entries: Entry[] = []; } /** A dictionary is a reference work over words: headword entries * containing definitions. */ class Dictionary extends ReferenceWork { declare entries: (Entry & { headword: Word; definitions: Definition[] })[]; } /** A thesaurus is a reference work over word-relations. */ class Thesaurus extends ReferenceWork { declare entries: (Entry & { synonyms: Word[]; antonyms: Word[] })[]; } /** An encyclopedia is a reference work over referents: entries * containing whole articles — containment straining, one rung * before the overflow. */ class Encyclopedia extends ReferenceWork { declare entries: (Entry & { article: Article })[]; } /** A concordance is the index of a corpus promoted to a book: * every word, every occurrence. */ class Concordance extends ReferenceWork { declare entries: (Entry & { headword: Word; locators: Locator[] })[]; } /** An almanac is a reference work over the year. */ class Almanac extends ReferenceWork {} /** A gazetteer is the atlas's index grown into its own book: * a reference work over place names. */ class Gazetteer extends ReferenceWork { declare entries: (Entry & { place: Name; locators: Locator[] })[]; } /** A directory is a reference work over people and institutions. */ class Directory extends ReferenceWork {} /** * A subject is a reference work whose entries POINT at whole books — * the overflow crossing from containment to reference. Double-faced: * functions IN a catalogue (an entry that gets you to a region of the * library) and serves AS one (indexing the books once you arrive). * The reification of the subject-of role: books share a subject-of * value; that population, canonicalized and labeled, IS this genre. */ class Subject extends ReferenceWork { declare entries: (Entry & { synopsis: Synopsis; link: Link })[]; heading!: SubjectHeading; scopeNote?: ScopeNote; /** May write and enforce specifications on its books (see Textbook). */ specifications: unknown[] = []; } /** The library catalogue is the subject of all subjects, itself a * book in the library it catalogues — the fixed point. */ class LibraryCatalogue extends Subject {} /* --------------------------------------------------------------------- * SERIALS — works that arrive forever. * ------------------------------------------------------------------- */ /** A serial is a work issued in parts without a planned end. */ class Serial extends Work { issues: Issue[] = []; } /** An issue is a writing: one arrival of a serial, made of articles. */ class Issue extends Writing { articles: Article[] = []; } /** A journal is a scholarly serial (the diary's homonym-twin). */ class Journal extends Serial {} class Magazine extends Serial {} class Newspaper extends Serial {} /** A series gathers separate works under a series title. */ class Series extends $Chemical { title!: Title; works: Work[] = []; } /* ===================================================================== * THE PUBLICATION STRATA — the four levels, in trade vocabulary. * Work (above) → Edition → Copy. Attribution is work-level (Byline); * signing is copy-level (Autograph): "a signed COPY," never * "a signed work." * =================================================================== */ /** The imprint: publisher, place, date — the work's civic registration. */ class Imprint extends $Chemical { publisher!: Publisher; place!: Name; date!: string; } /** An edition is a published embodiment of a work. */ class Edition extends $Chemical { of!: Work; imprint!: Imprint; editionStatement?: Phrase; } /** A copy is a single exemplar of an edition — the thing on the * shelf, with its accession number, its coffee stain, and, if a * reader carried it to the author, its autograph. */ class Copy extends $Chemical { of!: Edition; accessionNumber?: Name; autograph?: Autograph; } /** An autograph is the author's inked act upon one copy. Not the * byline: the byline attributes every copy; the autograph blesses one. */ class Autograph extends $Chemical { by!: Author; on!: Copy; } /* ===================================================================== * THE CATALOGUE SIDE — the librarian's record anatomy. * =================================================================== */ /** A record is a full catalogue entry: the cataloguer's fixed anatomy. */ class Record extends Entry { titleStatement!: { title: Title; subtitle?: Phrase }; statementOfResponsibility?: Byline; editionStatement?: Phrase; imprint?: Imprint; /** Collation: the physical description — pages, illustrations, height. */ collation?: Phrase; subjectHeadings: SubjectHeading[] = []; /** Tracings: every other heading under which entries for this work file. */ tracings: Name[] = []; callNumber?: CallNumber; } /** An authority record is the canonical record of an agent's name, * with see-references from variants — the catalogue-native ancestor * of the canonical autobiography. */ class AuthorityRecord extends Record implements Canonical<Agent> { of!: Agent; variants: Name[] = []; seeAlso: CrossReference[] = []; } /* ===================================================================== * EXCLUDED ON PRINCIPLE * Page, leaf, folio, recto, verso, running head — facts about how * text is poured into a vessel; view artifacts of the runtime, * not the catalogue. * =================================================================== */ export { $Chemical, Text, Word, Phrase, Sentence, Line, Stanza, Paragraph, Passage, Section, Name, Title, SubjectHeading, Byline, Heading, Locator, PageReference, CallNumber, Url, Reference, Citation, CrossReference, Link, Quotation, Epigraph, Dedication, Definition, TopicSentence, Synopsis, Abstract, Blurb, Caption, ScopeNote, Figure, Table, Agent, Person, CorporateBody, Author, Editor, Translator, Compiler, Publisher, Work, Writing, Document, Essay, Article, Letter, Chapter, Preface, Foreword, Introduction, Afterword, Appendix, Entry, List, TableOfContents, Index, Glossary, Bibliography, Notes, Errata, Cover, TitlePage, Book, Part, Novel, Monograph, Treatise, Textbook, Manual, Anthology, Proceedings, Biography, Autobiography, Memoir, Diary, ReferenceWork, Dictionary, Thesaurus, Encyclopedia, Concordance, Almanac, Gazetteer, Directory, Subject, LibraryCatalogue, Serial, Issue, Journal, Magazine, Newspaper, Series, Imprint, Edition, Copy, Autograph, Record, AuthorityRecord, }; export type { Canonical, Derivative, Catalogue }; TSEOF cd /mnt/user-data/outputs && npx tsc --noEmit --strict --target es2020 srt_semantics_of_books.ts 2>&1 | head -30; echo "EXIT: $?"

Both documents are above. What the excavation actually changed:
The Reference correction, grounded. The essay dissects the reference across every habitat — catalogue card, citation, footnote, bibliography entry, index entry, hyperlink — and the same anatomy appears every time: identification coupled with location. A reference has a name and a locator; it is not a kind of name. In code: Reference extends Text { title?, name?, imprint?, locator? }, with Citation, CrossReference, and Link as its genuine children.
Your signature instinct, vindicated by the domain's own formal model. Librarianship stratifies the book into work → expression → edition → copy, and the professional literature's own example of a copy-level fact is the author's autograph. So the code now splits what I had wrongly fused: Byline (a phrase attributing the work, printed in every copy) versus Autograph (an act upon one Copy, { by: Author, on: Copy }). The ELM librarian's "signature" turns out to be, in domain terms, a byline whose name resolves through an authority record to a canonical biography — the domain had every part except the traversability, and the ELM adds the link.
The inventory is now the domain's, not my sample of your examples. ~95 classes: the text spine with verse (Line, Stanza) and Passage; the full referential family; the compression ladder as justified canonicals (Definition → Caption → ScopeNote → Treatise-as-canonical-of-a-Subject); Figures and Tables as embedded non-Text; a third root, Agent (Person, CorporateBody, Author — flagged as the deliberate role-as-class stretch); the complete book anatomy in canonical order, cover to colophon, with the List family (ToC, Index, Glossary, Bibliography, Notes, Errata) unified by the domain's own convergent homonym, entry; the genres; the serials; the publication strata; and the cataloguer's Record with its real fields — heading, statement of responsibility, collation, tracings, call number — plus AuthorityRecord. Every extends carries its warrant as a one-line ordinary sentence; if the sentence sounds wrong read aloud, the extends is wrong.
The three edit passes are recorded truthfully in the revision history — coverage, correctness, language. The skeleton compiles clean under --strict. The HTML plate is now a draft behind and I'd rather redecorate it after your read than before. Where did I still miss?

Here’s another good example and I need you to detach from what you started with if it doesn’t appear in the language. You decide that name is important. Is that a domain term from writing? Sure I guess it’s the name of the author.? the name of the book is the title. Name is not a word in the domain writing. It is a type and then there are specific names relevant to writing, and so it might deserve to be something here.
What else in your domain is not actually bound to the domain? What words have more specific types that don’t change your meeting? What words could be used in other places they’re not about writing and still function? Look at how much time you been talking about the name of a book when most human beings do not talk about the name of the book. I would say someone saying what’s that book called. What’s the title of that book are more natural. Certainly in writing we would use title. Yes if you were looking for the domain language for writing, I would say we should probably favor what is used in writing over in speech? Presented files
Both documents revised — three more passes (4–6), recorded truthfully in the history. The generalization of your two examples became a principle with its own section:
The two strata (new §9). Every term now carries provenance. Imported generals — name, work, list, entry, record, table, copy, series, agent — function unchanged far outside writing; the domain uses but does not own them, and they're exactly the classes that derive from $Chemical without apology, which is what your original charter asked for before we knew why. Native words — title, headword, byline, chapter, blurb, colophon, errata — carry the domain. And the resolving pattern: the domain rarely coins from nothing; it takes a general and gives it a native specialization that doesn't change the general meaning. Title under Name, Writing under Work, catalogue-record under record, signed-copy under copy. The strata boundary is an inheritance boundary.
The withdrawn warrant. The "name of that book" justification is formally withdrawn in the essay as prior-bent, replaced by the written register's evidence: the title page's first field, the operand of every citation format. §2 was rebuilt entirely from attested written formulas — "No part of this publication may be reproduced," "hereafter cited as," ibid., "see above," the bookseller's "signed first edition," the dictionary's own front matter.
What the research found that construction hadn't. Lexicography defines the headword as the lemma — a canonical word form, such as the nominative singular of a noun, which represents the word's whole family — so Headword extends Word implements Canonical<Word> is the compression ladder's true bottom rung, in the domain's own words. Ibid. ("in the same place") proves the reference's anatomy by subtraction: identification elided, locator surviving alone. And "hereafter cited as" is canonization performed inside the document — the writer designating a short form to stand for the full citation.
Code: provenance tags throughout, Headword, PenName, and Sense added (the dictionary now carries lexicography's real microstructure — headword and numbered senses), the copy homonym noted, clean under--strict. The plate is now two drafts behind; I'd like your verdict on the strata before I redecorate it.

2026-07-17T22:57:47.158780Z
Claude
You're right to stop me, and I'm going to take the interview option seriously: synchronize first, build after you've said yes. Let me show you what I now understand, concretely enough that you can falsify it.
The generalized lesson: build by function, in closed layers — the minimum structure that works, then one shell at a time. Frequency was never the criterion; load-bearing is. First base before balk. Headword and Sense were balk: real words, wrong layer, and reaching for them instead of Chapter meant I hadn't found the game yet. And your digital-book point reframes everything: we're representing the information of a book, not the physical object — so cover-as-first-chapter isn't weird, it's the honest reading.
Here are the layers as I understand them — each one a complete little dictionary:
Layer 0 — the seed. Book: a sequence of chapters. Chapter: a body of text. Closed, functional, done.
Layer 1 — finding your way. Title: what a book or chapter is called. Author: who wrote it. Summary: a chapter's short account of itself, owned by the chapter. Location: where the chapter sits, owned by the chapter. Cover: the part carrying the book's title and author. Table of contents: an aggregate over the chapters — each entry just reads out a chapter's title, summary, location. Nothing in the ToC is authored twice.
class Chapter { title; summary; location; text }
class Book    { cover; contents; chapters: Chapter[] }
class Cover   { title; author }
class TableOfContents { /* derived from chapters */ }
Layer 2 — the other parts. Foreword, Epilogue, Index, Synopsis join — and now the base-type question. Your evolution story: chapters turned into these; the irregular parts are specializations of chapter, and Cover is the first "chapter" of the file. I can follow that cleanly. The one domain word I can offer as an alternative base is matter — the domain's own term for everything in a book that isn't nothing: front matter, body matter, back matter. But I'm genuinely unsure it works as a countable class, and Chapter-as-base may be more beautifulbecause it carries the evolution.
Layer 3 — closure under books. Reference: for now, just a string holding another book's title, since in a library closed under books every link lands on a book. Author becomes a reference to an autobiography; Subject (the field on a cover) a reference to a subject-book. Subject (the book type): a book whose own chapters describe the subject and whose table of contents catalogues other books rather than only its chapters.
Layer 4 and out — genres that force specialized parts (dictionary and friends), only when a layer actually demands them.
Questions before I write anything:
Base type for parts: Chapter as the base (Cover, Foreword, Index extend it), or a named base — and if named, does matter sit right with you or is it a stretch?
Location in a digital book: is it simply ordinal position in the sequence?
You said chapter summary but book synopsis — same concept at two scales with two native words, or should one word rule both?
In a Subject, do member books live inside it, or only their references (titles), with the library resolving them?
Is the table of contents itself one of the parts in the sequence, or a property of the book that stands outside the sequence?
Say yes/no/otherwise to these and I'll write the layered file exactly to your answers.

Research done — the elicitation literature converges on a few disciplines I'll hold myself to: teach-back (I state my understanding, you amend until satisfied), laddering (questions that move deliberately up, down, and across the hierarchy), forced-choice with concrete instances rather than open prompts, and using your terminology once uncovered, never substituting mine. One decision per question. After each round of answers I edit the code and the plate, then return with five more.
Round 1:
Q1 — teach-back on the construction principle itself. My understanding: we build in closed layers, where each layer is the minimum dictionary that functions — a term earns entry only when some capability at that layer demands it and nothing already present provides it. Test instance of my own: Index cannot enter at Layer 1, because finding-by-topic isn't yet a capability anything needs; ToC covers finding-by-structure, and Index waits until a layer whose function it uniquely serves. Do I understand correctly?
Q2 — the base type for parts (laddering up). Three options, consequences stated: (A) Chapter is the base; Cover, Foreword, Index are specialized chapters — carries your evolution story, and in a digital book reads honestly: the cover is the file's first chapter. Consequence: book.chapters is the whole sequence, and ToC iterates it filtering by kind. (B) A neutral base with a domain name. Candidates I can find: Division (printers genuinely speak of the divisions of a book) or Matter (front/back matter — but it's a mass noun and resists counting). Consequence: Chapter stays pure, but we pay a naming tax. (C) No base; Book holds typed fields (cover, foreword, chapters, index) — no unified sequence. Which — or something you see that I don't?
Q3 — code proposal, agree/disagree. Layer 1 as I'd write it:
class Chapter { title; summary; location; text }
class Book    { cover: Cover; chapters: Chapter[] }
class Cover   { title; author }
// ToC: computed view over chapters — reads out each
// chapter's {title, summary, location}; stores nothing of its own
The falsifiable claim: the ToC owns no authored content — it's an aggregate, full stop. Counterexample to test the edge: real tables of contents sometimes curate their wording. In the ELM, may a ToC entry ever override a chapter's own title or summary — yes or no?
Q4 — boundary probe on parthood. Candidate criterion: a part is anything in the book's reading sequence with its own text body; title and author are fields, not parts. Two instances I chose to test it: an epigraph standing before chapter one — part or field? The synopsis on the back of the book — part in the sequence, or field of the book? (Accept/reject the criterion, and rule on the two.)
Q5 — predictive guess at the closure move. For closure under books with no index mechanism yet: type Ref = string (a book's title); cover.author: Ref pointing at an autobiography; cover.subject: Ref pointing at a subject-book. My generalization guess one step beyond what you said: a chapter'slocation (within the book) and a Ref (outward to another book) are the same functional concept — where following takes you — and a Subject's contents can mix both because entries only ever need a destination. Do you agree they unify, or must within-location and outward-reference remain distinct types?

Claude
Adjusted. Five questions, full sentences, book vocabulary only, one decision each.
Question 1. What is the smallest thing that still counts as a book? Choose one: (A) a sequence of chapters, nothing else. (B) a sequence of chapters plus a title. (C) a sequence of chapters plus a title plus an author.
Question 2. Every part of a book needs a common base type so a book can hold parts it doesn't know about yet. The publishing guides' own collective phrase is "the parts of a book," which suggests the base type could be named Part — but books also use Part for "Part One, Part Two," a group of chapters, so the name would collide. Choose one: (A) the base type is Chapter, and cover, foreword, and index are special kinds of chapter — in a digital book the cover is simply the first chapter of the file. (B) the base type is named Part, accepting the collision with "Part One." (C) you supply a better word from the world of books.
Question 3. Which of these must every book have, and which are optional? Answer like "title: must, cover: optional": title / author / cover / chapters / table of contents.
Question 4. Proposed stub: a Chapter has a title, a summary, a location, and its text. A Book has a cover and its chapters. A Cover has the book's title and the book's author. The table of contents is built from the chapters' titles, summaries, and locations. Is this structure right — yes, no, or amend?
Question 5. The library is closed under books, so an author is represented by a book about that author — the autobiography. Until there is a lookup mechanism, should the author property on a cover simply hold the title of that autobiography as a plain string? Yes or no.

2026-07-18T18:34:49.536856Z
Claude
Book — the whole bound work; the file itself in a digital library.
Chapter — the standard part a book is read through; a body of text with a title.
Section — a division within a chapter, under its own heading.
Part — a group of chapters ("Part One"); also the generic word in "the parts of a book."
Volume — one physical book of a work too large for one binding.
Cover — the outside of the book; bears title and author.
Title — what a book or chapter is called.
Title page — the page that formally states title, author, and publisher.
Table of contents — the front list of the book's parts with their locations.
Index — the back list mapping topics to locations.
Entry — one item in a table of contents, index, catalogue, or dictionary.
Heading — the line of text standing at the head of a section or entry.
Reference — a pointer from one text to another: identification plus location.
Citation — a reference that credits a source.
Cross-reference — a reference to another place in the same book ("see chapter 3").
Footnote — a note at the foot of the page, usually carrying a citation.
Bibliography — the back list of all references a book makes to other books.
Synopsis — a short account of the whole content, standing in for it.
Foreword — an introductory part written by someone other than the author.
Appendix — a supplementary part standing after the chapters.

2026-07-18T18:38:58.467308Z
Claude
The evolutionary words — the ladder where each rung is made by cataloguing the rung below:
Text — raw written content; no name, no author, no address.
Passage — a stretch of text; still nameless, but excerptable.
Document — the first self-standing unit: text with a title, addressable as a whole.
Chapter — a document that belongs to a book.
Book — a document whose content catalogues a list of documents (its chapters).
Anthology — a book cataloguing documents by many authors.
Subject — a book cataloguing whole books instead of chapters.
Library — the catalogue of subjects; itself representable as one more book.
The closure answer: Document is the thing everything is closed under. A chapter is a document. A book is a document — one whose body is a catalogue of documents. A subject is a book, so also a document. The whole library is documents cataloguing documents, all the way up, and "book" names the rung where a document's content becomes a catalogue.
And the cover realization is the same move as excluding page, one level up. Page was view; cover is also view — the rendering a reader-facing surface gives to the book's own facts. What the representation actually holds is just those facts: the title, the author reference. An e-book proves it: there is no cover in the file's essence, only a record of title and author that any view may paint as a cover. So the pre-physical book is: a document carrying its own record (title, author, subject) and a catalogue of its member documents. The cover, the spine, the jacket are what different views do with the record — the representation stays clean underneath.
This also cleans the earlier stub: Cover disappears as a class; its fields move onto Book directly, which is where they always belonged.

2026-07-18T18:44:57.333222Z
Claude
Candidates for the primordial representational unit — the thing a book and a chapter both are, stated in language's own terms:
Writing — "a writing." A chapter is a writing; a book is a writing composed of writings; the library's whole content is one closure of writings. Native, countable, scale-free.
Text — the linguist's universal: any stretch of language, at any scale, is a text. A book is a text made of texts. Maximum generality, but carries no sense of unit-hood — a half sentence is also text.
Composition — a composed unit of language. Names the operation as well as the thing: compositions compose into compositions. The closure operation and the closed thing share one word.
Piece — "a piece of writing": genuinely used at every scale (a piece can be a paragraph, an essay, a book). Informal register, but exactly the scale-free unit word.
Account / Record — a written rendering of something; carries aboutness (every account is an account of something), which the others lack.
Document — the earlier candidate, now visibly wrong-flavored: it imports standalone-ness and near-authorship, which belong to the book layer, not below it.
Analysis of why closure matters, in this representation:
A layer is closed when the operations that build its things take that layer's things as inputs and yield that layer's things as outputs — so the layer can stand alone as a universe of representation, needing nothing from outside itself. Set theory is a level because sets of sets are sets: membership never exits the universe. The library is a level because books cataloguing books are books: reference never exits the closure — even the library itself gets a book.
The onion, read this way, is a stack of closures, each defined by what becomes total at that layer:
Language layer — closed under composition: compositions of language are language. Nothing has an author or an address yet; there is only the woven thing. This is the pre-book universe, and its unit is whichever word above is chosen.
Book layer — closed under cataloguing: books cataloguing books are books. What becomes total here: author and subject. Every thing at this layer has an author and a subject (chapters by inheritance from their book); below this layer the question "whose? about what?" has no slot to land in. The book is the minimum unit where an author makes sense — so the book layer is exactly the closure at which aboutness and responsibility saturate the universe.
Library layer — the book layer's own self-catalogue; closed already by the book layer's closure, which is why the library needs no new primitive, only its own book.
So the structural claim: each layer's identity is its closure operation plus what saturates at it. Composition saturates language; authorship and subject saturate books. The representational unit below the book should therefore be the word that carries composed language unit and nothing more — no author, no address. By that test the strongest candidates are Composition (operation and thing unified) and Writing (native and countable), with Text as the maximally general floor beneath both.

2026-07-18T18:47:15.684636Z
Claude
Restating to check understanding:
The set-theory move is not "find a layer below books." It is: introduce one primitive, and then everything at the level is that primitive — in set theory, numbers, functions, relations are all just sets. So at this level: book, chapter, section, subject, library are not different types. They are all the same recursive thing at different positions, the way a set and its member sets are all sets. One structure, one-to-one with all of them:
class Composition {
  title: string;
  author?: string;      // own, or inherited from the containing composition
  content: Text | Composition[];
}
A chapter is a composition whose content is text, catalogued by another composition.
A book is a composition whose content is compositions — cataloguing is just what containment looks like at this level.
A subject is a composition whose contained compositions are books.
Authorship enters with the level (cataloguing and authorship arrive together) and flows downward: a composition either states its author or inherits it from its container — which matches: pieces have an author, inheritable from the top.
Why composition may be the right word where document fails: a document is a physical artifact — a file, a paper. Butcomposition is the noun of an authored act — every composition grammatically implies a composer, so the word itself carries exactly the property that saturates at this level. Document has to be given an author; a composition comes with one.
Is this the structure meant — one recursive authored unit, with book/chapter/subject as positions in it rather than separate types? Yes, no, or amend.

2026-07-18T19:01:47.669146Z
Claude
Corrected — the closure applies to the fields themselves. Nothing on a composition may point outside the level: the author is not a string of a person's name, it is a reference to another composition — the autobiography, the book that stands in for the author inside the closure. Persons never enter the representation; their autobiographies do. Same for subject.
type Ref = string;   // a composition's title, until a lookup mechanism exists

class Composition {
  title: string;
  author?: Ref;      // → an Autobiography: the composition that stands in for its author
  subject?: Ref;     // → a Subject: the composition that catalogues what this is about
  content: Text | Composition[];
}
Autobiography: the composition where the loop closes — its author reference resolves to itself.
The earlier Agent/Person root was outside-the-closure thinking and is deleted: there is nothing at this level but compositions and references to compositions.
Author and subject arrive with cataloguing and inherit downward: a chapter's author resolves through its containing composition unless it states its own.
Amend or confirm; on confirm, the code file and plate get rebuilt around Composition as the single primitive.

2026-07-18T21:17:21.656009Z
Claude
Understood — I had collapsed two levels into one. Restated:
Composition level: a composition is composed of compositions; part–whole is intrinsic — by definition, not by an added field. That structure is what lends itself to authorship: a whole whose parts belong to it is the shape authorship will need. But the level itself cannot represent an author, because nothing exists at this level that could stand in for one.
Book level: cataloguing introduces compositions that stand in for things — the autobiography for an author, the subject-book for a topic. Only now does author become expressible, as a reference, because only now is there something for the reference to land on.
// Level 1 — composition: part–whole, nothing else
class Composition {
  parts: Composition[];   // a composition is composed of compositions
}
class Text extends Composition { }   // a leaf: composed of nothing further?

// Level 2 — the book level: cataloguing makes reference possible
class Book extends Composition {
  title: Ref;        // ?
  author?: Ref;      // → an Autobiography
  subject?: Ref;     // → a Subject
}
One question: does title also wait for the book level — since a title exists to be referenced by a catalogue, and pure compositions need no names — or do compositions already carry titles below the book level?
